En las imágenes se ve cómo avanza unos cuantos metros, pero después se atora en el lodo.\nUnos militares intentan sacar el jeep, pero fue imposible, el\nmandatario decidió recorrer unos kilómetros a pie. De acuerdo con la\nAFP\n, López Obrador, y su gabinete de seguridad, tuvieron que sortear rocas y árboles que tapaban el camino, así como bordear la zona de derrumbes.\nEl mandatario estuvo acompañado de\nLuis Cresencio Sandoval, secretario de la Defensa Nacional (Sedena); José Rafael Ojeda Durán, secretario de la Marina (Semar), Rosa Icela Rodríguez, titular de Seguridad (SSPC); así como con Jorge Nuño, secretario de Comunicaciones y Transportes (SICT).\nAMLO camina unos kilómetros para llegar a Acapulco. (Foto: AFP)\nAMLO platica con damnificados de Otis\nEn el camino, López Obrador dialogó también brevemente con habitantes que habían salido a pie del puerto o de los pueblos cercanos a buscar a sus familias ante la ausencia total de comunicaciones y transporte en Acapulco.\nLos testimonios de esas personas son muestra de la fuerza devastadora de Otis.\n“Se sintió muy fuerte, nos dejó en shock porque ni de la casa pudimos salir, mucha gente estuvo buscando refugios, pues los ríos crecieron mucho”, dijo a la AFP Israel Pérez, panadero de 21 años. “Parecía el sonido de monstruos que venían de otro lado, que están furiosos”, añadió.\n“Esto nos afecta mucho, porque salimos a todos los pueblitos (a vender el pan)”, dijo.\nAMLO en su intento por llegar a Acapulco.\n\nEric Hernández acompañaba a una familiar a una cirugía en una clínica de Acapulco cuando golpeó el huracán Otis.\nTras el impacto, el joven de 24 años salió caminando del puerto a su pueblo para ver que su familia estuviera bien.\n“Tuvimos que cerrar las puertas con lo que encontramos para que no volaran los cristales. Nos tocó ver cómo arrastraba carros y postes. El piso de la clínica se movía. Una cosa muy fea que yo nunca había vivido en el tiempo que tengo aquí”, dijo Hernández en medio del fango que dejó el huracán.\nLee:\nCentros de acopio por el huracán Otis en Guerrero: dónde están y qué puedes llevar\nEl joven añadió que\nuna vez que pasó el huracán tomó la decisión de caminar. En la ciudad observó que había tiendas saqueadas, con gente que se peleaba por las cosas.\n“Acapulco quedó en desastre total”, dice.\nPor más de 10 horas, la Autopista del Sol quedo cerrada por los deslaves que ocasionaron las lluvias de la Tormenta Tropical ‘Otis’, la cual toco tierra siendo Huracán categoría 5.\nTras varias horas en carretera y unos kilómetros a pie, el mandatario llegó a su destino cerca de las 19:30 horas.\nEl gobierno de Guerrero, mediante un comunicado, dio a conocer que Evelyn Salgado, gobernadora de la entidad, se reuniría con él p\nara hacer el recuento de los daños provocados por Otis. (AP Photo/Martin Zetina)\nAdd AP News on Google\nAdd AP News as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google.\n\nShare\nShare\nFacebook\nCopy\nLink copied\nPrint\nEmail\nX\nLinkedIn\nBluesky\nFlipboard\nPinterest\nReddit\nRead More\nUpdated [hour]:[minute] [AMPM] [timezone], [monthFull] [day], [year]\nAdd AP News on Google\nAdd AP News as your preferred source to see more of our stories on Google.\nShare\nShare\nFacebook\nCopy\nLink copied\nPrint\nEmail\nX\n\nLinkedIn\nBluesky\nFlipboard\nPinterest\nReddit\nMEXICO CITY (AP) — Mexico’s Maya Train rail project opened partially to the general public Saturday, amid hours-long delays and huge cost overruns.\nPassengers waiting for the twice-daily train to leave the resort of Cancun were left waiting on the platform for about five hours before being able to board. Officials apologized for the delayed and said it was due to trains being “reconfigured.”\nSome passengers napped on the floor of the concrete platform. Some — many self-declared supporters of President Andrés Manuel López Obrador — cheered when the train finally appeared in Cancun.\nThe train running in the other direction from the Gulf coast colonial city of Campeche was also delayed for hours, because only one side of the planned double rail line is finished. Officials estimated it would take about 5 1/2 hours to travel the 290 miles (473 kilometers) from Campeche to Cancun.\nRelated Coverage\nMexico’s president inaugurates first part of $20 billion tourist train project on Yucatan peninsula\nWorries abound that Mexico’s Maya Train will destroy jungle\nMexican officials admit secrecy-shrouded border train project had no environmental impact study\nMeanwhile, the cost of the project has soared from original estimates of around $8.5 billion, to as much as $28 billion.\nThe 950-mile line,\ncalled the Maya Train\n, is meant to connect beach resorts and archaeological sites. However, only about one-third of it — the 290-mile (473-kilometer) stretch that President Andrés Manuel López Obrador inaugurated with dignitaries and the press Friday — has even been partly finished.\nOfficials pledged the rest of the line would be ready by the end of February.\nBut even on the part López Obrador inaugurated Friday\n, just a single line of a planned double-line track has been finished, meaning one train has to wait on a siding while another passes.\nThe stretch running between Campeche and Cancun is about one-third of the entire project and covers the least controversial portion of the route, which crosses many environmentally sensitive areas.\nRead More\nA first-class ticket on one of the two trains from Cancun to Merida, the most popular stop, will cost the equivalent of $68. A first-class bus covers the route in about the same time and costs about $58, with buses leaving about every half hour from the city centers, rather than remote train stations on the outskirts.\nThat led to questions about whether the train will ever cover its operating costs, much less its construction budget.\nMexico’s army, which operates the train and built part of the railway, did not respond to a request for comment about the delays or cost overruns.\n\nSign up for Morning Wire:\nOur flagship newsletter breaks down the biggest headlines of the day.\nEmail address\nSign up\nBy checking this box, you agree to AP's\nTerms of Use\nand acknowledge that AP may collect and use your data pursuant to our\nPrivacy Policy\n.\nIn comments Friday, López Obrador acknowledged, “Yes, things are lacking (on the train), of course,” and predicted it would take “three years, four years” for the train system to begin covering its operating costs under the best scenario.\nAsked how much the construction cost of the project would be, the president said: “I don’t know ... I don’t have the exact figure.”\nOriginally projected to cost $8.6 billion, by now $22.7 billion has been assigned to the project, and Treasury Secretary Rogelio Ramírez told local media the final cost would be around $28 billion.\nUnlike the remaining two-thirds of the Maya Train, the part of the line inaugurated Friday already had an old train line running over much of the route. Many of the still-unfinished parts were cut through the jungle and built over sensitive, relic-filled cave systems, drawing objections from environmentalists.\nLópez Obrador has raced to finish the Maya Train project before he leaves office in September, rolling over the objections of ecologists, cave divers and archaeologists.\nThe train runs along the Caribbean coast and\nthreatens extensive caves\nwhere some of the oldest human remains in North America have been discovered. Because of the region’s limestone geology, it is riddled with caves that carry most of its water.\nWhile officials have touted the train as utilitarian transport for freight and local residents, its only real source of significant income would be tourists. However, given its frequent stops, unwieldy route and lack of feasibility studies, it is unclear how many tourists will actually want to buy tickets.\n“The train won’t help residents get to work or school, and besides, it’s very expensive,” SELVAME, a coalition of groups opposing the project, said in a statement Friday. “The train runs through the jungle, filling cenotes (sinkhole lakes) and underground rivers with concrete, without any studies.”\nLópez Obrador has tried to rush through the\nproject\nby exempting it from normal permitting, public reporting and environmental impact statements, claiming it is vital to national security.\nIn November 2021, his administration issued a broad decree requiring all federal agencies to give automatic approval for any public works project the government deems to be “in the national interest” or to “involve national security.”\n____\nFollow AP’s coverage of Latin America and the Caribbean at", "excerpt_sha256": "12befd1f0d10756729f3909a1366f54049d2c7b9d8d9d4697a88e6ecd075e4ca", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0030-E011", "source_id": "SRC-83fb76ce9917da4f", "url": "https://sidof.segob.gob.mx/notas/docFuente/5749268", "title": "Decreto relativo a la revisión de la Cuenta Pública Federal 2022", "publisher": "Secretaría de Gobernación / Cámara de Diputados", "raw_sha256": "a0acebae3465547116cf497b8ed15675cc967fd299e9f1212c8c28c8a098fc33", "fact_summary": "The federal legislature's review of the 2022 public account reported revenues above budget, spending of 7.595 trillion pesos, debt below the authorized amount and lower than the prior year as a share of GDP, and completion of 2,153 audits. "DECRETO relativo a la revisión de la Cuenta de la Hacienda Pública Federal correspondiente al Ejercicio Fiscal 2022\nDECRETO\nrelativo a la revisión de la Cuenta de la Hacienda Pública Federal correspondiente al Ejercicio Fiscal\n2022.\nAl margen un sello con el Escudo Nacional, que dice: Estados Unidos Mexicanos.- Poder Legislativo Federal.-\nCámara de Diputados.\nLA CÁMARA DE DIPUTADOS DEL HONORABLE CONGRESO DE LA UNIÓN, CON FUNDAMENTO EN\nLO DISPUESTO POR LA FRACCIÓN VI DEL ARTÍCULO 74 DE LA CONSTITUCIÓN POLÍTICA DE LOS\nESTADOS UNIDOS MEXICANOS, DECRETA:\nPRIMERO.\nLa Cuenta Pública de la Administración Pública Federal se integró, presentó y fiscalizó en\ncumplimiento de los mandatos constitucionales y legales aplicables.\nSEGUNDO.\nEn 2022 los ingresos presupuestarios del sector público ascendieron a 6,602,829.7 millones\nde pesos, cifra superior en 430,194.6 millones de pesos con relación a la prevista en la Ley de Ingresos de la\nFederación.\nEste resultado se explica por el registro de mayores ingresos petroleros respecto al programa por\n394,508.6 millones de pesos, así como por el incremento de los ingresos no tributarios no petroleros que se\nubicaron 101,721.3 millones de pesos por encima del programa.\nTERCERO.\nEl gasto neto total del sector público presupuestario ascendió a 7 billones 595 mil 307.9\nmillones de pesos, lo que significó un incremento real de 5.1% respecto al observado el año previo.\nCon relación al PIB el gasto neto total ejercido en 2022 representó 26.7%, 1.5 puntos porcentuales más\nque el estimado originalmente, y 0.5 puntos porcentuales mayor al registrado en 2021.\nDel total, 75.2% correspondió a gasto programable, mediante el cual se generan los bienes y servicios que\n\nla población demanda, y el restante 24.8% fue gasto no programable, que se asocia a la cobertura de\nobligaciones como el pago de participaciones a entidades federativas -previsto en la Ley de Coordinación\nFiscal-, el costo financiero de la deuda pública y los adeudos de ejercicios fiscales anteriores.\nLos ramos administrativos con mayor ejercicio de recursos de lo aprobado fueron: en Energía\n(143 mil\n955.7); Turismo (116 mil 470.6); Hacienda y Crédito Público (49 mil 674.3); Defensa Nacional\n(37 mil 93.3);\nEducación Pública (17 mil 11.7); y Medio Ambiente y Recursos Naturales (16 mil).\nCUARTO.\nEl endeudamiento neto interno y externo autorizado al Ejecutivo Federal en la Ley de Ingresos\nde la Federación 2022 ascendió en conjunto a 927,520.0 millones de pesos y el ejercido en el mismo año fue\nde 884,811.8 millones de pesos, el cual resultó de la diferencia entre los financiamientos obtenidos por\n5,711,700.1 millones de pesos y las amortizaciones efectuadas por 4,826,888.3 millones de pesos, y fue\nmenor al autorizado en 42,708.2 millones de pesos (4.6%).\nEl Saldo Histórico de los Requerimientos Financieros del Sector Público (SHRFSP), la definición más\namplia de la deuda se ubicó en 49.4% del PIB, cifra que resulta inferior a la estimada de 51.0% del PIB y a la\nregistrada en 2021 de 50.8% del PIB.\nQUINTO.\nDe\nlos Informes Individuales de Auditoría de la Fiscalización Superior de la Cuenta Pública 2022,\npresentados por la Auditoría Superior de la Federación a esta Cámara de Diputados, se constató la práctica\nde 2,153 auditorías, por lo que la Auditoría Superior de la Federación cumplió con el 100% de las auditorías\nconsideradas en el Programa Anual de Auditorías para la Fiscalización Superior de la Cuenta Pública 2022.\nDe las revisiones practicadas por la Auditoría Superior de la Federación, 88 corresponden a entes\nvinculados con funciones de Gobierno, 140 a Desarrollo Social, 163 a Desarrollo Económico y 1,762 a Gasto\n\nFederalizado.\nAsimismo, la Auditoría Superior de la Federación determinó Recuperaciones por 32 mil 894 millones de\npesos, de los cuales 3 mil 128.1 millones de pesos son Recuperaciones Operadas, de manera que restan\n29\nmil 765.9 millones de pesos que estiman como recuperaciones probables o en su caso, montos por aclarar.\nNo obstante, debe considerarse que, de esas posibles recuperaciones 22 mil 892.1 millones de pesos\ncorresponden a las entidades federativas, alcaldías, municipios y entes que ejercieron Gasto Federalizado.\nDe esto se desprende que la proporción a solventar o aclarar para el Sector Público Federal, es de 6 mil\n873.8 millones de pesos.\nDe esto se desprende que la proporción a solventar o aclarar para el Sector Público Federal, representa\n0.09% respecto del Gasto Neto Total.\nSEXTO.\nSe estima que la labor de fiscalización practicada por la Auditoría Superior de la Federación a la\nCuenta de la Hacienda Pública Federal 2022 cumplió con los criterios de objetividad, normativos y\nmetodológicos para la selección, análisis y presentación de las auditorías practicadas.\nSÉPTIMO.\nEsta Comisión destaca que la aprobación del Dictamen no suspende el trámite de las acciones\npromovidas por la Auditoría Superior de la Federación de manera que quienes sean presumiblemente\nresponsables, sean sancionados en apego a la normatividad vigente o en su caso, presenten las aclaraciones\no solventaciones correspondientes.\nOCTAVO.\nSe instruye a la Auditoría Superior de la Federación para que continúe el procedimiento jurídico\nde responsabilidades relacionadas con la Cuenta Pública 2022, así como a remitir a esta Cámara de\nDiputados la información actualizada sobre la recuperación o aclaración de recursos por parte de los entes\nfiscalizados.\n\nNOVENO.\nSe considera necesario, para la mejora del desempeño y la reducción de las áreas de riesgo\nidentificadas, que los entes auditados fortalezcan sus procesos de planeación en las actividades sustantivas y\nrefuercen el control interno mediante la optimización normativa y reglamentaria.\nDÉCIMO.\nLa Comisión de Presupuesto y Cuenta Pública coordinará la instalación de una mesa de trabajo\nen la que se ponderará y dará seguimiento a las recomendaciones realizadas por la Auditoría Superior de la\nFederación a la Cámara de Diputados, sobre diversas modificaciones legales para incorporar disposiciones\nque mejoren los procesos de los entes auditados. A dicha mesa de trabajo se convocará también a los\nintegrantes de las Comisiones de Transparencia y Anticorrupción y de Vigilancia de la Auditoría Superior\nde\nla Federación.\nSALÓN DE SESIONES DE LA CÁMARA DE DIPUTADOS DEL HONORABLE CONGRESO DE LA\nUNIÓN.\n-\nCiudad de México, a 11 de febrero de 2025.\n- Dip.\nSergio Carlos Gutiérrez Luna\n, Presidente.-\nRúbrica.- Dip.\nJosé Luis Montalvo Luna\n, Secretario.- Rúbrica.", "excerpt_sha256": "437ec46c7377dba2b63b6148889b5c90087e86b9071214a02931ac724421d72f", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0031-E003", "source_id": "SRC-ab7b114efc0a22f2", "url": "https://www.france24.com/en/americas/20231030-search-and-recovery-continue-in-mexico-as-otis-death-toll-climbs", "title": "Search and Recovery Continue in Mexico After Otis", "publisher": "France 24", "raw_sha256": "d0a32eb621dbd041d10196f58b85e3f85a1b732bdcbed8022ce01b18be51b090", "fact_summary": "Tras el huracán Otis, que golpeó Acapulco como categoría 5, la ayuda gubernamental comenzó a distribuirse varios días después; residentes reportaron falta de agua, alimentos y asistencia. To watch this content, you may need to disable it on this site.\nTry again\nCover image: Locals remove debris left by the passage of Hurricante Otis in Puerto Marques, Guerrero state, Mexico on October 28, 2023. © Rodrigo Oropeza, AFP\n01:13\n\"We are going to fight for ourselves,\" Margarita Carmona told AFP after chopping up fallen trees along with her son-in-law and other residents in the Puerto Marques neighborhood.\nThey, like many in this port city and tourist haunt of 780,000, grew tired of waiting for government aid, which only began being distributed Saturday -- days after\nCategory 5 Otis roared ashore\nand left local landmarks, built over decades, look like they had been bombed out.\nPresident\nAndres Manuel Lopez Obrador\nflew over the disaster area and held meetings with officials in charge of the relief operation, local media reported.\nAn aerial view of damages caused by Hurricane Otis in Acapulco, Guerrero state, Mexico on October 28, 2023. © Rodrigo Oropeza / AFP\nAfter the\nhurricane\nhit Wednesday, a feeling of abandonment gripped residents, including Julian Matadama, a 52-year-old construction worker, who banded together with other locals to clear debris.\n\"We are in need of help,\" he said.\nBut after widespread water, power and telecommunications outages, they face a tough road ahead.\n\"There is no way for you to feed yourself, there is no way to drink water,\" added a fisherman, standing next to him.\nAnother setback\nThough the city is far from its tourism heyday in the 1950s and '60s, when it attracted Hollywood stars and jet setters, its beaches still attract visitors -- or at least they did before Otis.\nA man walks past a damaged area in the aftermath of Hurricane Otis in Acapulco, Mexico on October 29, 2023. © Felix Marquez / AP\nOmar Flores' small family restaurant is miraculously still standing, though it has lost its roof and the kitchen is devastated.\n\nNow he fears it will be months before visitors return to the port.\n\"It's going to be another hard blow for all the people who work on the beach,\" he said. \"Just thinking about how much money is needed to get back on your feet, where do we get it from?\"\nOtis isn't the first setback for Acapulco.\nIn 1997, the region was hit by Hurricane Paulina, a Category 4 storm that killed more than 200. Acapulco managed to recover, only for worsening drug violence to later drive away high-income tourism.\nBut with heavy investment, and sporting events like the tennis Mexican Open, the city was once again on the rise in recent years. In February, the government announced a hotel occupancy rate of 75 percent.\nNow, beaches are littered with debris and dead animals.\nTaken by surprise\nThe hurricane's death toll climbed Sunday as five more people were confirmed dead in Coyuca de Benitez, north of the city.\nSome 36 people were still missing, authorities said at midday.\nRelatives of hurricane victims seek information outside a morgue in Acapulco on October 29, 2023. © Felix Marquez / AP\nThe count of victims has been slow after the storm collapsed telecommunications systems, which have been gradually returning over the weekend.\nThe World Meteorological Organization has described the hurricane as \"one of the most rapidly intensifying tropical cyclones on record,\" exceeded in modern times only by another Pacific hurricane, Patricia, in 2015.\nThe speed with which Otis intensified took the government and weather forecasters by surprise, leaving little time to issue warnings and prepare residents for its "BATCH-0031-E013", "source_id": "SRC-ea995c3326971637", "url": "https://www.journalofdemocracy.org/online-exclusive/can-mexicos-next-president-control-the-military", "title": "Can Mexico's Next President Control the Military?", "publisher": "Journal of Democracy", "raw_sha256": "4cc62b7274067c47025175c2bc68fa030a466c180df6894241985f6c0f26a3ac", "fact_summary": "AMLO asignó a las fuerzas armadas poderes, presupuesto y fuentes de ingresos crecientes, extendiéndolas a seguridad, puertos, aduanas, infraestructura, bancos, aeropuertos y hoteles. Disolvió la policía federal, creó la Guardia Nacional con 80% de personal militar y promovió su control por el Ejército; la Suprema Corte declaró inconstitucional ese traslado en 2023, pero el gobierno aún no había implementado la decisión y promovía legislación para reforzarlo.", "question_ids": ["3B.2", "3B.4", "4B.4", "4B.5", "4B.9", "5B.5", "5B.6", "7B.6", "7B.7", "8B.2", "8B.3", "8B.5", "8B.6"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "Incluye la sentencia y la situación institucional de 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "Las decisiones de expansión militar y la falta de implementación de la sentencia se atribuyen al gobierno de AMLO.", "limitations": ["El artículo señala que no había casos claros de generales desobedeciendo órdenes civiles; la dependencia militar también se originó parcialmente antes de AMLO."], "start_unit": 2, "end_unit": 3, "locator": "extracted HTML block 2 through extracted HTML block 3", "exact_excerpt": "Online Exclusive\nCan Mexico’s Next President Control the Military?\nThe country’s military brass has a larger role governing Mexico than at any time in the past eighty years. It’s creating a dangerous dependency that won’t be easy to break.\nCan the generals be reined in?\nBy\nWill Freeman\nMarch 2024\nO\nn June 2, Mexico will hold the biggest elections in its history. Voters will choose a new president, 628 members of Congress, and thousands of state and local officials. But there is at least one set of politically consequential decisionmakers that will not appear on the ballot: Mexico’s armed forces. Since taking office in 2018, President Andrés Manuel López Obrador (AMLO) has assigned the military sweeping new powers, an ever-larger budget, and new revenue streams. He has also stood by as generals and officers have sought to insulate themselves from judicial investigations. It’s no exaggeration to say the armed forces now have sway over security policy, tax collection, and the day-to-day operation of critical parts of the state.\nAMLO’s efforts to remake Mexico’s electoral and judicial institutions, which remain only partially implemented after pushback from the courts, have inspired heated debate at home and abroad. Yet his empowerment of the armed forces, which has sparked less backlash, has resulted in more significant, and likely more lasting, change to Mexico’s institutions. For now, Mexico remains a democracy, despite its flaws. It’s far from a “tutelary” regime, in which generals call the shots and civilians only pretend to rule. While generals have successfully lobbied lawmakers to pass legislation and seem to have influenced AMLO’s decisionmaking, there are no cut-and-dry cases of military leaders defying civilian orders. Despite pro-AMLO\nrhetoric\nfrom some of the generals, they do not act as political kingmakers or, seemingly, aspire to that status.\nBut the military has become as important to governance as any of the country’s civilian bureaucracies — and in some policy areas such as domestic security, perhaps even more so\n.\nThe armed forces have taken charge of police work nationwide, the operation of Mexico’s ports and customs offices, and the construction of key infrastructure. Mexico’s next president — whether she’s AMLO’s protégé, Claudia Scheinbaum, who leads in the polls, or opposition candidate Xóchitl Gálvez — will find it difficult to govern without the armed forces’ active support.\nMexico’s new normal, a civilian government that is chronically dependent on the military, does not threaten democracy in the familiar ways. There is no risk of a military coup in Mexico. Rather, it creates a new set of dilemmas for democracy regarding the collection and use of intelligence, the independence of the judiciary, and the provision of public goods. Military dependency and its associated dilemmas will likely be AMLO’s most enduring legacy, not the social justice–inspired “\nfourth transformation\n” he promised.\nA Substitute State\nBy the late 2000s, few could have predicted Mexico’s military dependency. It came about gradually, as successive civilian governments relied on the military to do the jobs of underperforming civilian institutions. The country had plenty of problems to worry about in the last half of the twentieth century, but an increasingly powerful military was not one of them. From the 1940s to the 1990s, the civilian-led authoritarian Institutional Revolutionary Party (PRI) maintained a stable deal with the armed forces: As long as the generals stayed out of politics, the military could manage its own affairs. Even as the army took on some internal security tasks, successive PRI governments made the arrangement stick by keeping the armed forces “corrupt, poorly trained, poorly equipped, and totally useless,” in the words of former foreign minister Jorge Castañeda.\nThe arrangement — autonomy in return for subservience to civilians — remained in place as long as the armed forces stayed weak. But as Mexico democratized, governments began relying on the military to take on the tasks of the state, and with these came power. In 2000, an opposition presidential candidate, Vicente Fox (2000–2006), finally beat the PRI. But unlike new democratic presidents in Central and South America who feared their coup-prone militaries, Fox did not try to curb the military’s autonomy. On the contrary, to confront rising violence fueled by drug trafficking, he appointed a retired army general as Mexico’s top federal prosecutor and\ndispatched\neighteen-thousand soldiers to fight the cartels. Fox’s successor, Felipe Calderón (2006–12), expanded the military’s role in policing and declared war on the cartels in 2006. The armed forces showed no signs of winning, but military spending increased. To their credit, Fox and Calderón did try to build civilian-led federal police forces, but they remained too small and susceptible to high-level corruption to stem the violence.\nMexico’s next president, Enrique Peña Nieto (2012–18) of the PRI, promised to rely less on the armed forces for internal security, but once in office, he continued growing the military’s power. While neglecting the federal police, he greenlit a sixfold increase in the size of the military police. Meanwhile, the army-run defense ministry flexed its muscle by lobbying lawmakers to pass a national-security law of its own design in 2017, making permanent its role in domestic policing (Mexico’s Supreme Court declared the law unconstitutional in 2018).\nBy that time, the relationship between civilians and soldiers had started to shift. At the invitation of state governors, active-duty army personnel from the defense ministry began\n\nfilling\nkey government posts and supplanted police in states like Sonora on the Arizona border. Repeated military deployments took the pressure off governors to build competent state police forces, and some state governments doled out contracts to army-affiliated private firms, several of which were later exposed as shell companies.\nAlthough civilian officials were relying on the military for public security before AMLO became president in 2018, he has done the most to entrench military dependency and extend it to new areas of government beyond public security. This is an about-face. Before assuming the presidency, López Obrador had vocally opposed the militarization of public security. Once he took office, however, he began calling the armed forces “incorruptible” and expanded the military’s power like no president before him.\nAMLO dissolved the federal police force and created the National Guard to replace it in 2019. This new force, at first formally under the control of a civilian ministry, received 80 percent of its personnel, much of its leadership, and extensive training from the military. In 2022, AMLO rallied his MORENA party’s lawmakers to place the National Guard under the army’s control through 2028. In 2023, the Supreme Court ruled the move unconstitutional and ordered a return to civilian control. AMLO has yet to implement that decision and recently pushed back with\nproposed legislation\nthat would shore up the army’s control and expand the National Guard’s role in investigating crime.\nAMLO has also used presidential decrees to put the navy in charge of seaports and customs offices — the latter generates US$60 billion annually, or more than 15 percent of Mexico’s tax revenue — and dispatched the army to build and operate multibillion-dollar infrastructure projects (and\ndirect\na share of the proceeds to military pensions). He has put the armed forces in charge of constructing\nhundreds of new\nbranches of a state-owned bank that distributes cash transfers to older adults. Add to all that the airline, airport, and luxury hotels which have rounded out the military’s budding investment portfolio. In the decade-and-a-half between 2003 and 2018, the armed forces’ budget roughly doubled. It took AMLO only six years in office to double it again.\nNo one can say with certainty why AMLO chose to embrace the armed forces. But according to my interviews with current members of his party and ex-government officials, it is likely that he and a circle of top generals put aside their mutual distrust to forge a marriage of convenience. The president recognized that if he relied only on the small circle of trusted allies he brought with him into government, he would accomplish few of his goals, particularly building big new infrastructure. After\nfiring tens of thousands of civilian bureaucrats\nin the name of austerity, AMLO came to see the armed forces as a cost-effective, disciplined, and, above all, loyal substitute. Generals who positioned themselves close to the president and took charge of new portfolios also stood to gain.\nThe Perils of Military Dependency\nOne risk of Mexico’s military dependency comes with the armed forces’\ncontrol\nof intelligence-gathering, which raises the possibility of military leadership shaping, rather than merely informing, civilian decisionmaking. Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Ecuador, Peru, and Uruguay all maintain civilian-led intelligence agencies separate from their armed forces — a legacy of their democratic transitions. AMLO has pushed Mexico in the opposite direction. Retired and active-duty generals or their family members now run all federal intelligence-gathering institutions.\nThe armed forces can and do use their intelligence-gathering powers for good, tracking down narcotraffickers and exposing state officials who collaborate with them. But bad uses are also conceivable — and perhaps inevitable. In October 2022, the hacker group Guacamaya leaked thousands of official defense-ministry documents, revealing that the army had compiled\ndossiers\non protesters and journalists, categorizing them as pro- or antigovernment, and had tapped into the encrypted private communications of a\nWashington Post\nreporter investigating alleged extrajudicial killings by army personnel. Leaked emails showed that army higher-ups had\nrequested\nreports on cabinet members’ positions on the military and maintained files on dozens more politicians.", "excerpt_sha256": "735c31351cb10eda16ae7f62713cab4d915123ed0fde8c11dfd6d40fd91ee998", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0032-E002", "source_id": "SRC-cc6454689011bcb0", "url": "https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/Documents/Pobreza_Multidimensional_2022.pdf", "title": "Informe de la pobreza multidimensional en México, 2022", "publisher": "CONEVAL", "raw_sha256": "1251448088af9b7a6b3d92bac9efb6a57f7afbe3e62304bbf9e6499b567abe5c", "fact_summary": "La pobreza multidimensional disminuyó de 41.9% de la población en 2018 a 36.3% en 2022, pero la pobreza extrema aumentó en términos absolutos de 8.7 a 9.1 millones de personas. De acuerdo con la Ley General de Desarrollo So-\ncial (2022), el espacio del bienestar económico se expresa por medio del ingreso corriente total per\ncápita, mientras que el de los derechos sociales está representado por las carencias por acceso a\nlos derechos de educación, salud, alimentación nutritiva y de calidad, vivienda y seguridad social.\n\n\nAl analizar los resultados de la pobreza multidimensional 2022 y compararlos con los de 2018, sobre-\nsalen las diferencias con respecto a la incidencia de las carencias sociales en la población. Así, a\nnivel nacional, la población que presenta una o más carencias sociales, pero cuyo ingreso es su-\nperior a la línea de pobreza por ingresos, es decir, la población vulnerable por carencias, pasó de\n32.7 a 37.9 millones de personas (26.4 % a 29.4 %, respectivamente). Esto supone que el aumento en el\ningreso de los hogares no se tradujo en una reducción de sus carencias promedio.\n\n\n\n                                                                                             Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social   25\n\nEl dato anterior refleja que el acceso a los derechos sociales sigue siendo un reto pendiente para la\npolítica pública de desarrollo social, la cual requiere de un avance integral en las dimensiones de la\npobreza. Al observar la suma de carencias que presenta cada persona (índice de privación social),\nse identificó que en 2018 el 68.3 % de la población (84.6 millones de personas) presentó al menos una\ncarencia social y el 20.2 % de la población (25.0 millones de personas) presentó al menos 3 carencias\nsociales. En 2022, esta situación fue de 65.7 %, (84.7 millones de personas) para la población con al\nmenos una carencia y en aquellas con al menos 3 carencias pasó a 24.9 % (32.1 millones de personas).\n\n\nAl interior de cada decil de acuerdo con el ingreso corriente total per cápita, las carencias sociales\nse encuentran presentes en cada uno de estos segmentos de población. Esto deja en evidencia\nque, a menores ingresos en el hogar, mayor es el número de carencias sociales y, por el contrario, a\nmayor ingreso, menor es la presencia de carencias (gráfica 4).\n\n\nGráfica 4. Entre 2018 y 2022, cuatro de las\nseis carencias registraron una contracción en su incidencia en la población, mientras que el rezago\neducativo y la carencia por acceso a servicios de salud presentaron, por el contrario, incrementos9\n(gráfica 5).\n\n\n\n\n9\n    Al analizar la significancia estadística de estos cambios se identifica que todos los cambios son significativos (p=0.02), con excepción del correspondiente al rezago educativo.\n\n\n\n\n     26            Informe de la pobreza multidimensional en México, 2022\n\nGráfica 5. estimaciones del CONEVAL con base en datos de las ENIGH 2018 y 2022.\n\n\n\n\nRezago educativo\n\nCon respecto al indicador de rezago educativo, este experimentó ajustes normativos y metodológi-\ncos como resultado de la reforma al artículo 3.° constitucional en 2012 y a la Ley General de Edu-\ncación en 201410 (DOF, 2012a; CONEVAL, 2019). Estos cambios incorporaron el nivel medio superior\ncomo parte de la educación obligatoria. Por esta razón, para efectos de la medición multidimensio-\nnal de la pobreza y sus ajustes técnicos de 2018, se incluyó el componente del indicador de rezago\neducativo, el cual identifica a la población de 22 años o más nacida a partir de 1998 sin educación\nmedia superior completa. Con ello se inició el monitoreo de este grupo de población, el cual reportó\npara 2022 que el 36.7 % de la población no cuenta con educación media superior (gráfica 6).\n\n\nEn cuanto a la tasa de inasistencia se identifica un cambio de 4.5 puntos porcentuales (pp en ade-\nlante), lo cual señala que, en 2022, el 52.1 % de la población de 16 a 21 años no había concluido el\nnivel medio superior educativo (preparatoria). Esto evidencia la brecha educativa entre el nivel obli-\ngatorio establecido en la Constitución y los niveles educativos alcanzados por la población.\n\n\nDentro del indicador de rezago educativo, el componente que presentó una disminución fue para\nla población nacida antes de 1982 que no cuenta con la primaria completa, el cual pasó de 25.3 %\na 23.7 % de 2018 a 2022. Sin embargo, se debe tener en cuenta que la reducción se debe, principal-\nmente, a la mortalidad de la población adulta mayor en situación de rezago, y no únicamente a un\navance educativo. Durante este periodo, se registró una disminución en la población de este grupo\nde 1,041,877 personas. Al analizar las variaciones del componente en cuestión, este tuvo un incre-\nmento de 27,342 personas. De haberse mantenido el mismo volumen poblacional, de 2018 a 2022, el\nvalor para este componente sería igualmente de 25.3 %.\n\n\n10\n     La Ley General de Educación de 1993 fue abrogada y dio pasó la Ley con nombre símil a través del Decreto por el que se expide la Ley General de Educación y se abroga la\n     Ley General de la Infraestructura en 2019 (DOF, 2019b).\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                                Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social          27", "excerpt_sha256": "48b3e9720d0f246fc023c5e6545df7a0e1e20dcea8bb16490635e768d4d2b75c", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0032-E003", "source_id": "SRC-cc6454689011bcb0", "url": "https://www.coneval.org.mx/InformesPublicaciones/Documents/Pobreza_Multidimensional_2022.pdf", "title": "Informe de la pobreza multidimensional en México, 2022", "publisher": "CONEVAL", "raw_sha256": "1251448088af9b7a6b3d92bac9efb6a57f7afbe3e62304bbf9e6499b567abe5c", "fact_summary": "La carencia de acceso a servicios de salud aumentó de 16.2% en 2018 a 39.1% en 2022. servicios de salud presentó la mayor variación entre los indicadores\nde la medición multidimensional de la pobreza a nivel nacional, con un aumento total de 22.9 pp al\npasar de 16.2 % a 39.1 % de 2018 a 2022, respectivamente.\n\n\nEsta carencia afecta principalmente a dos grupos. En primer lugar, entre las personas que tienen un\ntrabajo remunerado o ejercen una actividad económica de forma independiente (población ocu-\npada), pero que no cuentan con acceso directo a los servicios de salud. Este grupo pasó de 64.5 % a\n63.1 % (37.4 a 38.9 millones de personas). El segundo lugar, se concentra en la población desocupa-\nda y que carece de servicios de salud, la cual aumentó de 14.5 % a 38.5 % (véase gráfica 7).\n\n\nGráfica 7. Porcentaje de población con carencia por acceso a servicios de salud, según situación de\nocupación, 2018 y 2022*\n                                           64.5             63.1\n\n\n\n                                                                                                                       38.5\n\n\n\n                                                                                                      14.5\n\n\n\n\n                             Población ocupada sin acceso directo a                      Población que no trabaja sin acceso a servicios\n                                       servicios de salud                                                   de salud\n\n                                                                             2018           2022\n\n*Para un mejor análisis de la información 2022 consulte las notas técnicas, disponibles en el Anexo I.\nFuente: estimaciones del CONEVAL con base en datos de la ENIGH 2018 y 2022.\n\n\n\n\n  28          Informe de la pobreza multidimensional en México, 2022\n\nAl analizar los componentes del indicador de la carencia por acceso a servicios de salud, se observa\nque gran parte de la variación porcentual podría estar asociada a los cambios en el sistema de sa-\nlud pública, sumados al inicio de la pandemia por COVID-19. A inicios del 2020, el Gobierno federal\neliminó el Seguro Popular y creó el Insabi. En este mismo periodo, el COVID-19 llegó a México, y cuan-\ndo se decretó como una emergencia sanitaria, la coordinación interinstitucional no fue la adecua-\nda (Cárdenas, 2022). Además, se identificó que la transición del Seguro Popular al Insabi, aunque\nbuscaba incrementar el tamaño de la población atendida, resultó en un menor gasto destinado a\nsalud proporcionalmente. Asimismo, el Insabi limitó la atención de enfermedades de alta especia-\nlidad. Como consecuencia, se registró que esta transición resultó en un menor acceso y un mayor\ngasto de bolsillo por parte de la población (CIEP, 2021). Esto podría estar relacionado con el hecho\nde que un porcentaje menor de la población declarara contar con afiliación o acceso a servicios\nde salud en 2022 en comparación con 2018. En la gráfica siguiente se observa que el porcentaje\nde población que reportó acceso al Seguro Popular fue mayor que el reportado para el Insabi y el\nIMSS-Bienestar, tras dos años de su desaparición (gráfica 8).\n\n\nGráfica 8. Para\n2022, dicho escenario cambió, dado que la población con esta carencia fue del 18.4 % en el decil X,\ny representó el 66.7 % de la población en el decil I. Es decir, se registró un incremento de 49.9 pp en\neste decil, con lo cual se observó que los primeros deciles fueron los mayormente afectados.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                                                                                                                    Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social                                                  29\n\nGráfica 9. participación de los\nprogramas de transferencias monetarias gubernamentales dirigidos a la población mayor de 65\naños. En 2018, el 61.7 % de este grupo de población no contaba con acceso a algún programa de\ntransferencias económicas; esta cifra se contrajo al 29.3 % en 2022. Es pertinente señalar que los pro-\ngramas sociales de transferencias monetarias gubernamentales dirigidos a este grupo de población\ntienen por objeto otorgar un recurso económico que permita a las personas cubrir sus necesidades\nbásicas. Sin embargo, para dar cabal atención al cumplimiento del derecho, se tendría que otorgar\nel acceso a las prestaciones de la seguridad social, tales como servicios de salud, créditos para la\nvivienda, etcétera.\n\n\nPor ello, es relevante señalar que la población ocupada sin acceso directo a la seguridad social en\n2022 disminuyó 1.4 pp en relación con 2018, lo que en términos absolutos se traduce en que el núme-\nro de personas con esta carencia pasó de 37.6 a 39.2 millones de personas (gráfica 10).\n\n\n\n\n  30                Informe de la pobreza multidimensional en México, 2022\n\nGráfica 10. estimaciones del CONEVAL con base en datos de las ENIGH 2018 y 2022.\n\n\n\n\nCarencia por acceso a la alimentación nutritiva y de calidad\n\nEntre 2018 y 2022, la carencia por acceso a la alimentación nutritiva y de calidad pasó de 22.2 % a\n18.2 %. Esto significa que la población que presentó esta carencia pasó de 27.5 millones a 23.4 mi-\nllones de personas. Los cambios en las cifras de esta carencia dan cuenta de una mejoría en la se-\nguridad alimentaria de los hogares, sin limitación en el consumo de alimentos. En este sentido, este\ncambio refiere a un incremento en el porcentaje de la población que reportó contar con alimentos\nen su hogar de forma continua y sin pasar hambre. En 2022, 85.2 millones de personas presentaron\nesta condición.\n\n\nEn lo que respecta a la carencia por acceso a la alimentación nutritiva y de calidad, el indicador\nidentifica a aquellos hogares que presentan: 1) inseguridad alimentaria moderada o severa;11 2)\nlimitación en el consumo de alimentos,12 es decir, las personas tuvieron un consumo limitado de\nalimentos; o 3) ambas condiciones. De forma general, se puede observar una mejora en cada uno\nde los componentes de la carencia, a excepción de aquellos hogares que presentan limitación en\nel consumo de alimentos (gráfica 12). Aunque en términos porcentuales no hubo cambios, con una\nincidencia en el 3.5 % de la población, en términos absolutos se registró un retroceso al pasar de 4.4\na 4.5 millones de personas con dieta pobre o limítrofe.\n\n\n\n\n11\n     De acuerdo con la Escala Mexicana de Seguridad Alimentaria (EMSA).\n12\n     La limitación en el consumo de alimentos de los hogares hace referencia a aquellos en que sus integrantes tuvieron una dieta pobre o limítrofe, considerando la frecuencia de\n     consumo y la diversidad de la dieta de doce grupos de alimentos, para aproximar la adecuación de nutrientes.\n\n\n\n\n      32              Informe de la pobreza multidimensional en México, 2022\n\nGráfica 12. Durante el periodo analizado, esta carencia presentó\nuna tendencia a la baja a nivel nacional del 11.0 % a 9.1 % (de 13.6 a 11.7 millones de personas). Cabe\nresaltar que el hacinamiento en las viviendas es el componente de la carencia que sigue siendo el\nde mayor incidencia en la población. Aún en 2022, se registran 7.4 millones de personas que habitan\nen viviendas sin cuartos suficientes para todos sus habitantes (gráfica 14).\n\n\nSi bien los porcentajes de población con esta carencia son los menores de entre los reportados\npara las seis carencias sociales, su relevancia radica en que la población que reside en viviendas\ncarentes en la calidad y espacios enfrenta mayores barreras sociales, económicas y posiblemente,\ngeográficas. Tal como lo describe el índice de marginación del Conapo (2021a), donde la población\nen viviendas con mayores valores para el índice de hacinamiento y para viviendas con piso de tierra\nes la que presenta más carencia en servicios.\n\n\nGráfica 14. Planned stations changed from 17 to 21 plus 14 stops, and the Army was designated to operate the train and receive its profits.", "question_ids": ["5B.1", "5B.10", "5B.6", "8B.1", "8B.2", "8B.3", "8B.5", "8B.6", "8B.7"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The evidence concerns project planning and implementation through March 2022, relevant to the project during López Obrador's 2023 administration.", "ruler_attribution": "The project was a stated priority of López Obrador, who inaugurated its construction and set its development objectives; A esa fecha, el costo de la obra era de entre 120 mil y 150 mil millones de pesos, de acuerdo con la ficha técnica de construcción presentada por el Fondo Nacional de Fomento al Turismo (Fonatur).\nSin embargo, en octubre de 2021, en una comparecencia en el Senado, el entonces titular del Fonatur, Rogelio Jiménez Pons, mencionó que “el costo pasó a 200 mil millones de pesos y el máximo será de 230 mil millones de pesos”.\nAl arranque de la obra, el primer mandatario señaló que el principal objetivo era “el desarrollo de infraestructura de una región tan olvidada”; 11 menses después, López Obrador agregó que la finalidad era “aprovechar que llegan muchos turistas a Cancún y que no sólo sea estar en la playa sino introducirse y conocer toda la región maya”.\nDe acuerdo con la Secretaría de Hacienda y Crédito Público (SHCP), en 2019 se otorgó un presupuesto inicial de 5 mil 820 millones de pesos; en 2020 la cifra ascendió a 2 mil 500 millones; para 2021, 34 mil 823 millones de pesos, y para este año fue de 63 mil 231 mil millones de pesos. En total, el Tren Maya ha recibido del gobierno 106 mil 374 millones de pesos.\nAhorros invisibles\n\nLa presentación oficial del proyecto por parte del Fonatur revela que el Tren Maya abarca poco más de mil 500 kilómetros en ocho tramos.\nEl trazado original por segmentos era el siguiente: 1. De Palenque a Escárcega, 2. Escárcega-Calkiní, 3. De Calkiní a Izamal, 4. Izamal-Cancún, 5. De Cancún a Playa del Carmen, 6. Playa del Carmen-Tulum, 7. Tulum-Bacalar y 8. De Bacalar a Escárcega.\nEn junio de 2019, el Fonatur informó que la ruta del tren tendría modificaciones entre las estaciones de Valladolid y Cancún, que representaba una reducción de 55 kilómetros y un ahorro de 5 mil 500 millones de pesos. Pero en abril de 2020 se informó que éste sería modificado debido al hallazgo de cenotes y vestigios arqueológicos.\nEn agosto de 2021, el Fonatur comunicó que el tren no pasará por el centro de Campeche para economizar 2 mil millones de pesos, por lo que transitaría por la periferia de la ciudad que, además, debería construirse un libramiento elevado de 12 kilómetros. Apenas este 19 de enero, Javier May, actual director general del Fonatur, manifestó que el tren no se elevaría e iría sólo por tierra.\nUna semana después, el organismo encargado de su construcción notificó que ya no pasaría por Mérida, capital de Yucatán. Detuvo la construcción de la estación La Plancha, y ahora se realizará en la comunidad de Teya, a más de 60 kilómetros de donde se tenía planeada la parada principal. La decisión fue “para acortar el tiempo de construcción”, se explicó oficialmente.\nEl 11 de enero pasado, la Presidencia de la República informó que se hicieron cambios en el Fonatur, y Javier May Rodríguez fue designado el nuevo encargado.\nSeis días después, la dependencia dio a conocer que el transporte ferrocarrilero tendría otra adecuación: no pasará por la carretera 307 que corre de Cancún a Tulum, con el fin de evitar afectaciones de movilidad a la población local y a los turistas.\nEl Fonatur refirió que este trazo tendría que pasar por terrenos paralelos a la autopista, mismos que pertenecen a cadenas hoteleras, y debería expropiar 198 predios y pagar cerca de mil millones de pesos. Dos días después, el mismo organismo dijo que se realizarán nuevos estudios topográficos.\nModificar y transformar\nEl Fonatur y el Centro de Estudios de las Finanzas Públicas enviaron a la Cámara de Diputados, en septiembre de 2019, una ficha técnica, en la cual se planteó que se iban a percibir ingresos por 46 mil millones de pesos anuales por la operación del tren; de los cuales, 70 por ciento provendrían del transporte de carga y el restante provendría del servicio a pasajeros y turismo. Sería la SHCP la encargada de administrar estos recursos.\nEl 16 de marzo de 2021, Jiménez Pons declaró que todas las ganancias obtenidas por la operación del tren pasarían a manos del Ejército, pues la Secretaría de la Defensa Nacional será la encargada de su operación, y no sólo los segmentos 1, 6 y 7, como se planteó originalmente.\nTambién, la ficha técnica presentada por el Fonatur en 2019 indicaba que el tendido de la vía era simple; sin embargo. En su página de Internet, se indica que la mitad del trayecto es doble y que se hará la electrificación en más de 40 por ciento de la ruta al comienzo de la operación de todo el sistema\nEl itinerario, de acuerdo con la ficha enviada a diputados en septiembre de 2019, contemplaba 17 estaciones: Palenque, Chiapas; Tenosique, Tabasco; Calakmul, Escárcega, Xpujil y Campeche, en el estado homónimo; Maxcanú, Mérida, Izamal, Chichén Itzá y Valladolid, en Yucatán; Cancún, Puerto Morelos, Playa del Carmen, Tulum, Felipe Carrillo Puerto y Bacalar, en Q. Roo.\nLa última actualización del Fonatur indica que recorrerá cinco estados del sureste de México con 21 estaciones y 14 paraderos. Incluso, el organismo refiere en su sitio web que “aún puede modificarse el plan por razones operacionales”.\nLa Jornada\nsolicitó en varias ocasiones la postura del Fonatur respecto de los cambios en el trazo, las modificaciones presupuestales y el avance de la obra, sin obtener respuesta.\nA nterior\nS iguiente\nSubir al inicio del texto\n¿Quiénes somos?\n|\nContacto\n|\nPublicidad\n|", "excerpt_sha256": "746443d1945429ac9dc94cb6ea3d16bf9982e0b1127bd1797603083d991c7f94", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt calls it a priority project of the current government, not specifically a presidential-priority project."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0044-E009", "source_id": "SRC-3f04a442aa614979", "url": "https://www.amnesty.org/ar/wp-content/uploads/2023/11/AMR4174562023ENGLISH.pdf", "title": "Second UA: 108/2023 Mexico", "publisher": "Amnesty International", "raw_sha256": "b4138c9b884c97005c54e8ab0c140acd697c381c0e56150b68510f27114baf81", "fact_summary": "Amnesty reported that after a tidal wave and Cold Front 8 displaced the El Bosque community in Tabasco in November 2023, 69 people left a temporary shelter after alleged mistreatment and a full day without food or water; the relocation process promised in April had stalled, leaving some families homeless or returning to an uninhabitable area. Amnesty also reported inadequate water, health, electricity, and education services and criticized continued oil-production policy amid the climate crisis.", "question_ids": ["3B.7", "3B.9", "5B.6", "5B.9", "6B.1", "6B.10", "6B.2", "6B.3", "6B.6", "6B.8", "8B.7", "8B.9"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "Direct 2023 evidence, including events in November 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The appeal addresses López Obrador directly and evaluates the Mexican federal government's relocation, climate, and social-protection responsibilities.", "limitations": ["The allegations about shelter treatment are attributed to community reports received by Amnesty.", "The supplied document does not establish the final federal response after publication."], "start_unit": 1, "end_unit": 2, "locator": "page 1 through page 2", "exact_excerpt": "Second UA: 108/2023                      Index: AMR 41/7456/2023 Mexico              Fecha: 29 november 2023\n\n\n\n\nURGENT ACTION\nCLIMATE DISPLACED PEOPLE NEED A HOME URGENTLY\nOn 13 November, 69 people from \"El Bosque\" community (Tabasco) were forced to leave a temporary shelter in\nFrontera Village due to inhumane conditions and treatments. They were placed there temporarily after a tidal wave\nalmost destroyed their community. Without access to income and resources, they are unable to pay rent and are\ntherefore risking their lives through exposure to the extreme elements. The Mexican State must guarantee the human\nrights, including the rights to housing, adequate food, water and sanitation, and work and adequate standard of living,\nof the inhabitants of El Bosque, and immediately provide a participatory and fair relocation.\n\nTAKE ACTION: WRITE AN APPEAL IN YOUR OWN WORDS OR USE THIS MODEL LETTER\n\n                                                                                 Andrés Manuel López Obrador\n                                                                                           President of México\n                                                                Plaza De La Constitución S/N Palacio Nacional,\n                                                                       Centro, Cuauhtémoc, Ciudad de México,\n                                                                                           México C.P. 06067\n                                Email: atencionciudadana@segob.gob.mx / alejandro.esquer@presidencia.gob.mx\n                                                                                            Tw: @lopezobrador\n\n\n\nMr. President López Obrador,\n\n\nI am writing to urge you to guarantee the human rights of the inhabitants of El Bosque community, in Tabasco. On 1\nNovember, they were evacuated due to a tidal wave caused by Cold Front 8 (Frente Frío 8) to a temporary shelter in\n“Frontera” village (12 km far from El Bosque). Most of the people lost their homes. Members of the community\nreported that on 13 November, they were mistreated by personnel of the temporary shelter, and they did not receive\nfood or water for a full day, which forced them to leave.\n\n\nSince then, most families of El Bosque have had to use their own scarce resources to rent a place, some others have\nborrowed rooms, and the people who do not have money to pay a rent are practically in situation of homelessness. In\ndesperation, some people had to return to their community, despite it being uninhabitable due to losses and damage\ncaused by from the cold front 8, which could worsen in the following weeks as severe cold fronts are predicted.\n\n\nI urge you to immediately guarantee human rights, including the rights to housing, adequate food, water and work\nand adequate standard of living, of the inhabitants of El Bosque, and to expedite as fast as possible the relocation\nprocess your government promised to the community on April, 2023, which must be participatory and fair. I also urge\nyour government to take adequate measures to reduce, mitigate and prevent the risks, losses and damage caused by\nthe climate crisis.\n\n\nYours sincerely,\n\nSecond UA: 108/2023                      Index: AMR 41/7456/2023 Mexico              Fecha: 29 november 2023\n\n\nADDITIONAL INFORMATION\nEl Bosque is a fishing community in the state of Tabasco, southern Mexico, affected by rising sea levels and coastal\nerosion intensified by the climate crisis. Since 2019, due to constant tidal waves and extreme weather events, the\ncoastline of El Bosque has lost 200 meters (about 656.17 ft), resulting in the destruction of homes and community\ninfrastructure. In April 2023, El Bosque community started negotiations with the Ministry of Agrarian, Territorial and\nUrban Development (Secretaría de Desarrollo Agrario, Territorial y Urbano) of Mexico and the Mexican National\nHousing Commission (Comisión Nacional de Vivienda en México) to be relocated. However, this process has stalled,\nleaving the community vulnerable to new extreme weather events, such as the torrential rains of Cold Front 8 on 1\nNovember.\n\nAmnesty International received information about the humanitarian crisis faced by nearly 64 families of El Bosque.\nPrior to the events of 1 November, the community had already lost more than 50 houses to rising sea levels, forcing\npeople to live in temporary shelters in the community or to be displaced. Also, before 1 November, the community\nreported that the water wells of El Bosque were already infiltrated by seawater, the healthcare services were neither\nconstant nor adequate; electricity service was intermittent; and due to the destruction of their schools, children were\nforced to attend classes in an inadequate infrastructure to guarantee their right to education. Following the events\nof 1 November, the community’s situation has only worsened.\n\nFrom 1 November, 69 people were in a temporary shelter provided by the Centla’s municipal government in the\n“Centro Recreativo del Municipio de Centla”. On the night of November 13, members of the community left the\nshelter, since they reported they were mistreated by the staff, and they did not receive food or water the whole day.\nSince then, most members of the community have spent their own money to pay a rent or have had to move to\nrelatives’ homes. However, there are people without resources to pay rent, or relatives to stay with, that have been\npractically left in a situation of homelessness, or have returned to the community despite the risk posed by new cold\nfronts.\n\nUnder international human rights law, the Mexican State must adopt measures to protect, prevent and reduce the\nrisks, losses and damages caused by the foreseeable and unforeseeable consequences of climate change. The\ndevelopment of robust mitigation and adaptation measures could reduce the number of people forced to move due\nto the consequences of climate change and prevent the aggravation of losses and damages generated by the\nclimate emergency. Unfortunately, the Mexican government has chosen to continue increasing oil production,\ninstead of generating solid commitments for a just energy transition to comply with the commitments made in the\nParis Agreement. The situation in El Bosque, and in Acapulco, Guerrero (city that on 25 October suffered the impacts\nof Hurricane Otis, the strongest storm ever to hit Mexico's Pacific coast), show the failure of the Mexican government\nto protect the most marginalized groups that bear the brunt of the climate crisis.\n\nThe Mexican State also has a duty to ensure that planned permanent relocations are a measure of last resort, and\nin cases such as El Bosque in Tabasco, where such internal displacement is imminent, it must comply with the\nframework of international obligations on internal displacement, which establish that a durable solution for displaced\npersons be achieved. This durable solution must ensure that displaced persons can enjoy, without discrimination,\nall their human rights, including security; an adequate standard of living, including access to food, water, adequate\nhousing, employment and elementary education; access to work and means of subsistence; and access to effective\nmechanisms for the restitution of housing, land and property of displaced persons, or to provide them with adequate\ncompensation.\n\n\n\n\nPREFERRED LANGUAGE TO ADDRESS TARGET: Spanish.\nYou can also write in your own language.\n\nPLEASE TAKE ACTION AS SOON AS that loads faster by showing text only?\nYes\nNo, don't ask again\n✕\nClose\nMexico: Public Accountability, Privacy Under Threat\nPrint\nDonate Now\nMay 24, 2023 2:01AM EDT\n|\nNews Release\nAvailable In\nEnglish\n\nEspañol\nMexico: Public Accountability, Privacy Under Threat\nIndependent Transparency Agency Left Paralyzed by Legislature, Government\nShare this via Facebook\nShare this via Bluesky\nShare this via X\nShare this via WhatsApp\nShare this via Email\nMore sharing options\nShare this via LinkedIn\nShare this via Reddit\nShare this via Telegram\nShare this via Printer\nClick to expand Image\nA police officer walks outside the offices of the National Institute for Transparency and Access to Information and Data Protection (INAI) in Mexico City, Mexico.\n© 2016 Archivo Agencia EL UNIVERSAL/RCC (GDA via AP Images)\n(Washington, DC) – President Andrés Manuel López Obrador and legislators from his party have effectively paralyzed the country’s independent transparency and data protection agency by blocking nominations to fill vacant seats on its board, Human Rights Watch said today. The Senate should move swiftly to fill the three vacant seats.\nThe National Institute for Transparency, Access to Information, and Data Protection (INAI) is the independent agency charged with enforcing transparency and data protection rules in\nMexico\n. It has played an important role in ensuring the right to privacy of ordinary Mexicans and ensuring that journalists and human rights defenders can obtain the necessary information to document and expose human rights violations and corruption. President López Obrador and\nlegislators from his party\nhave repeatedly\nproposed eliminating the agency\n, claiming it is unnecessary.\n“By intentionally paralyzing the transparency agency, the Mexican government is undermining Mexicans’ right to access public information and to make decisions about how their personal data is used,” said\n\nTyler Mattiace\n, Mexico researcher at Human Rights Watch. “Until the Senate names new transparency commissioners, Mexicans will have no way to easily appeal to an independent watchdog when the government denies them access to public information or when private companies misuse their personal data.”\nThe agency has the authority to require government agencies, political parties, labor unions, or other public bodies to comply with freedom of information requests from individuals or organizations. It also can require entities that hold personal data, such as government agencies, banks, or hospitals, to allow people to view, change, or eliminate data about themselves.\nIf a public entity refuses to respond to an information request, or provides incomplete information, the person who requested it can appeal to the agency, which can issue a legally binding decision compelling it to comply or pay a fine. The agency reviewed\nmore than 20,000 such cases\nand issued more than 11,000 decisions compelling public bodies to comply with requests between October 2021 and September 2022, according to its most recent annual report. The agency also takes judicial action to challenge laws that wrongly limit access to public information or threaten Mexicans’ right to privacy.\nThe agency’s seven commissioners are appointed by the Senate to serve seven-year terms. By law, the board needs at least five commissioners to meet, review cases, or issue decisions. Currently it has only four. No new commissioners have been appointed to replace the three who completed their terms in March 2022 and March 2023. Since March 31, the INAI board has\nnot had enough members to hold meetings\nor issue decisions. As of May 10, it had 3,947 pending cases, which it was unable to review.\nIn March, the Senate, which is controlled by legislators from President López Obrador’s coalition, voted to appoint two commissioners to fill the seats that had been vacant since April 2022. However, President López Obrador\nvetoed the appointments\n, alleging wrongdoing in the nomination process.\nIn April, senators from the president’s party\nvoted down a second proposal\nto fill one of the vacant seats. The Senate president, a member of the president’s party,\nintroduced a bill\nthat proposed eliminating the agency\nbut then retracted it\n. Opposition senators staged a protest,\ntaking over the Senate floor\nand refusing to discuss other issues until a commissioner was appointed. The Senate president refused and instead\nreconvened the Senate in a different building\nto\ncontinue voting on other bills\n. The Senate went into summer recess on April 30 without having appointed new INAI commissioners.\n\nA federal judge has repeatedly ruled, most recently on May 11, that the Senate’s failure to fill the vacant seats on the INAI board\nviolates Mexicans’ constitutional right to access public information\n. The judge ordered the Senate to name at least one commissioner to enable the agency to resume normal functions.\nThe agency has played a key role in enabling Mexicans to uncover serious human rights violations and government corruption schemes. For example, a 2015 ruling forced Mexico’s attorney general to release the records of its investigation into the 2014 disappearance of 43 students in Iguala, Guerrero, revealing major inconsistencies, including that key suspects had been tortured. Hundreds of freedom of information requests made through the agency have enabled activists to determine the location of more than\n2,000 mass graves\nacross Mexico. They have also allowed reporters to\nuncover corruption schemes\nthat diverted hundreds of millions of dollars from public services like health care and education.\nThe agency has also enabled Mexicans to access personal information about themselves in government records. Prior to a 2003 decision by the agency’s predecessor public hospital medical records were considered government property. Requests for medical records, pension contribution records, and other personal information held by the government continue to be one of the main types of information requests made through the agency.\nPresident López Obrador and legislators from his party have repeatedly proposed eliminating the agency and transferring its functions to government auditing agencies like the Ministry of Public Administration (Secretaría de la Función Pública), which is responsible for auditing the finances and performance of the executive branch of the federal government and reports directly to the president, or the Supreme Audit Office of the Federation (Auditoría Superior de la Federación), which is charged with auditing the use of the federal budget and reports to Congress. These institutions report to the same politicians and leaders they would be expected to hold to account, which means they would not serve as an independent check on government secrecy, Human Rights Watch said.\nPresident López Obrador\nissued an executive order\nin November 2021 designating all government infrastructure projects matters of national security, enabling officials to deny access to information about them. The transparency agency challenged the executive order before the Supreme Court, which voted to\noverturn it on May 18\nas a violation of the constitutional right to access public information. Hours after the Supreme Court ruling, President López Obrador\nissued a nearly identical executive order\n. The agency responded with\na formal complaint\n, which is now pending with the Supreme Court. However, the transparency agency cannot present a new legal case while its board lacks the five members required issue formal decisions.\nThe\nright to information\nis a recognized human rights norm. It includes the right of access to information held by public institutions and information on public affairs. Access to this information is also an important means of exercising the\nhuman right to participation\nin public affairs. Mexico should ensure easy, prompt, effective, and practical access to this information, including by establishing and guaranteeing procedures for obtaining the information and by processing requests for information in a timely manner.\nMexico should also ensure that every person can\n\nascertain what personal data\nis being processed and stored about them and why, and that they can request to view, correct, and in some cases erase that information, whether it is held by the government or private companies.\nMexico has\naccepted specific obligations\nto establish an independent authority with the powers to investigate and take legal action for violations of personal data protection laws. In addition, the Inter-American Commission on Human Rights’\nspecial rapporteur for freedom of expression has recommended\nthat an independent specialized agency be responsible for reviewing and deciding on government denials of requests for information.\n“The INAI has empowered journalists and activists in Mexico to uncover horrific abuses and massive corruption scandals precisely because it is independent from the government,” Mattiace said. “Eliminating the INAI and transferring its powers to an agency that reports directly to the politicians it is intended to hold to account, especially given Mexico’s long history of official secrecy and cover ups, would be the perfect recipe for abuse.”\nYour tax deductible gift can help stop human rights violations and save lives around
