In Mexico, this figure is likely to be -0.8% (a deficit) in 2022; given the slowdown of the economies of the U.S. and Mexico next year, we estimate Mexico’s current account will be at the same general level in 2023, around -0.9%. This deficit could be higher, but the flow of remittances will continue to help achieve a lower deficit next year.\nRemittances will continue to be a main driver of Mexico’s economy. This resource has grown exponentially since 2013 and become one of the most important sources of currency and income in Mexico—the second largest behind manufacturing. Remittances reached $58 billion in 2022, a historic high, and could hit $60 billion in 2023. Remittance income as a percentage of Mexico’s GDP will be more than 4.2% in 2023.\nFiscal Austerity\nMexico’s government will continue to practice fiscal discipline in 2023. Austerity has been a central feature of the López Obrador administration. However, there will be greater fiscal pressures in 2023 due to increases in social expenditures, particularly for pensions and cash transfers, and the president’s priority infrastructure projects. Public debt as a percentage of GDP has increased in recent years due to the primary deficit and the dynamics of the debt. Next year, the primary deficit will be around 0.2% of GDP and Mexico’s gross general government debt as a percentage of GDP will be above 50% of the GDP—still healthy but growing.\nFigure 4 —\nPrimary 2022 Economic Indicators and 2023 estimates\nSources\nBanxico and BBVA, 2022. Authors’ estimates for 2023.\nV. USMCA Disputes Persist\nTwo-and-a-half years after the USMCA entered into force, major areas of conflict have not changed and are likely to continue in 2023.\nUSMCA dispute settlement proceedings under Chapter 31 brought by Canada and Mexico against the United States challenging the U.S. interpretation of automotive rules of origin (“rollup” provisions) will likely continue into 2023, unless the panel rules before that time. If Mexico and Canada prevail, Mexican auto and parts manufacturers will gain additional flexibility in meeting the USMCA regional content rules (75% compared to 63.5% under NAFTA). If not, the gradual shift of automotive manufacturing from Mexico and Canada to the United States will continue and adjustments (e.g., to reduced industry employment) in Mexico will begin.\nU.S.-Mexican ongoing consultations over Mexico’s energy policies, which the U.S. (and Canada) believe are a violation of Mexico’s USMCA obligations to maintain foreign investor access to the energy sector under Article 32.11 (treatment equal to that provided to Mexico’s CPTPP partners) will result in panel proceedings. When the panel rules in 2023 it will likely confirm the U.S. and Canadian interpretation of the USMCA. President López Obrador will not change his policies, further poisoning the investment climate in Mexico, but any trade sanctions would not likely be imposed during 2023 pending further negotiations. Investment disputes will be a major irritant in U.S.-Mexico relations throughout 2023 (and 2024). Private investors will likely bring multiple investor-state claims against Mexico under NAFTA (until June 30, 2023), the USMCA, or under investment contracts with binding arbitration provisions.\nMultiple disputes over the USMCA’s environment provisions, in addition to Mexico’s failure to meet Paris Agreement criteria, its illegal fishing in the Caribbean, and its failure to protect endangered dolphins in the Gulf of California, will continue throughout 2023 and beyond. Lack of business access to clean, reliable electrical energy will discourage some new foreign investment in 2023 and thereafter.\nMexico’s refusal to authorize certain genetically modified corn traits developed in the U.S. to be sold in Mexico will continue. White corn is a culturally sensitive product in Mexico, and because of the interests of small farmers, López Obrador’s populism apparently views GMO corn as yet another affront to Mexico’s sovereignty. Bilateral discussions over the issue will continue into 2023 without resolution and could discourage support for Mexico among members of Congress from states that are major agricultural exporters to Mexico. Meanwhile, Florida interests are pushing the United States Trade Representative to open a Section 301 investigation of an “export targeting scheme” by Mexico to force Florida agricultural producers of seasonal fruits and vegetables out of business. Such action is unlikely on the part of the Biden administration but cannot be ruled out.\nVI. A Further Weakening of the Education and Health Sectors?\nHealth Overview\nHealth spending by Mexico’s federal government is low and increases slowly. Spending by all economic agents in Mexico grew from 5.67% in 2012 to 6.24% in 2020, just over a half percentage point in eight years. This uptick in spending did not approach the amount allocated by Mexico’s peers, who averaged 7.18% of their GDP on health care in 2019. At 3.3% of GDP in 2020, public spending on health care in Mexico is almost one point of GDP less than the cluster average of its peers, 4.24% in 2019. As a result, the people of Mexico had the second highest out-of-pocket health care expenditures among countries with similar economic conditions in 2020—2.42% of GDP or 38.8% of total health expenditures.\nSystematically low investments in public health care have limited Mexico’s human and physical capital compared to similarly situated countries. Mexico’s ratio of 1.21 nurses per doctor remained the same from 2012 to 2020, but was always lower than its peers’ average of 1.63 nurses per doctor in 2012 and 1.67 in 2020. The gap in care equipment is more dramatic: Mexico had 0.99 hospital beds per 100,000 inhabitants in 2020 (3% less than in 2010), but a cluster of similarly situated countries had far more, 5.1 hospital beds per 100,000 inhabitants.\nEven before the pandemic, some key health indicators began faltering. Mexico reported 13.8 deaths per 1,000 births in 2020, 3.9 more than the peer average. Furthermore, preventable deaths increased: Mexico was below the peer country average in 2012 (225 vs. 242 preventable deaths, respectively), but exceeded the average in 2019 (220 in Mexico vs. 182 for its peers). The percentage of children in Mexico under the age of one who completed the recommended vaccination schedule began to drop in 2016, when it fell to almost 93%, but it plummeted to 27.5% in 2021.\nSince 2014, the number of “care units” (or clinics) operating under the IMSS-Bienestar health care program has dropped; this has also been the case with care units operated by the Ministry of Health, which runs the most important network of public hospitals in the country. Just one year before the pandemic, in 2019, 65% of the budget for public health system infrastructure and equipment was not spent. Most of the unspent funds were earmarked for the expansion or remodeling of facilities and the acquisition of medical equipment for all public health systems operating in the country; the funds were not disbursed largely because the guidelines for doing so were unclear or nonexistent. Since 2017, all public health care systems have seen the effects of funding cuts, including shortages in everything from medicine to hospital beds, incubators, and operating rooms. Health infrastructure and equipment are outdated, and the numbers even show a decrease in the number of available hospitals and clinics.\nIn addition to the COVID-19 pandemic, the health sector in Mexico experienced other major challenges, such as institutional changes in health care programs, especially for people without formal access to federal assistance programs. The transition from Seguro Popular to Insabi occurred the same year as the pandemic, which worsened its impact. From the beginning, there was no expectation that Insabi would improve the benefits of Seguro Popular, since the new system did not have an operational design at the time of its implementation, nor did it develop it in subsequent years.\nSome operating indicators have shown worrisome deterioration, such as medical visits for the uninsured population, which began to fall in 2018 but plummeted through 2020 and has since continued to drop. From January to August 2022, Insabi granted 10.5 million medical visits. This is 29% lower than the number of visits in the same period in 2021, and 79% lower than the number of visits in 2018—before López Obrador took office.\nBy type of care, appointments for healthy people have seen the greatest drop in numbers: 2.2 million medical appointments were scheduled for healthy individuals between January and August 2022. This represents a 35% drop, or 1.2 million fewer visits, compared to the same period in 2021, and an 85% drop in visits compared to the same period in 2018.\nAnother worrying drop in medical visits involves those with chronic or degenerative diseases such as cancer, diabetes, or HIV/AIDS. Between January and August 2022, 2.4 million visits were reported, a reduction of 35% (or 1.3 million medical appointments) compared to the same period in 2021; and a drop of 72%, or 6.3 million medical appointments, compared to the same period in 2018. This unfortunate situation is due to fewer resources for catastrophic illnesses and to poorly written laws that do not specify how much should be spent for various purposes. The upshot is, a patient’s average out-of-pocket expenses for health care increased by 40.5% in 2020.\nThe maternal mortality rate also exploded during the pandemic, but was aggravated by other factors, such as the structural limitations of Mexico’s health care systems and institutional changes (i.e., the switch from Seguro Popular to Insabi and the financial weakening of Fonsabi). Before the pandemic, in 2019, rate stood at 31.1 maternal deaths per 100,000 births; during the coronavirus crisis, the rate increased to 53.1 maternal deaths per 100,000 births. One study reported that not all maternal deaths in Mexico were directly related to COVID-19; some were due to the limited availability of health care combined with the health of pregnant women at risk for serious complications.\n\nLife expectancy dropped worldwide by an average of two years due to COVID-19. But life expectancy in Mexico dropped by 4.3 years, going from 72.2 years in 2019 to 67.9 years in 2021.\nIn 2022, the government announced the closure of the Insabi program and launched a transition toward IMSS-Bienestar. IMSS-Bienestar is a smaller-scale program that does not have the budget, infrastructure, and equipment to serve the tens of millions of people in need of its services. In addition, it does not have tertiary care services and options for highly specialized medical treatments. Before being named for the challenge, IMSS-Bienestar also exhibited a deterioration in the medical services it offers. In 2021, medical visits under this program totaled 15.9 million—26% (or 5.4 million) less than the average for IMSS-Bienestar during the previous six years.\nFurthermore, the program lacks a detailed plan and strategy for expanding its capacity to care for patients; it is present in only 54% of the country's municipalities and in 19 out of 32 states. IMSS-Bienestar had had 80 hospitals since 2014; in 2020 it had 3,983 clinics, a lower number than in 2014.\nIn addition, in the last decade the number of doctors in the program has largely remained the same, while the number of beds and incubators has plummeted.\nFor 2023, the public health budget does not guarantee that progress can be made on the path to compensate for the deterioration. Officially, federal spending on health will be 3.9% higher than in 2022. However, the outlook is not so promising if resources whose destination is opaque or undefined (contributions to trusts or financial investments not yet specified) are subtracted. Aside from this ambiguous budget, resources that positively impact health will only grow 0.8% in 2023 (compared to an average growth of 14% between 2013 and 2018 under the previous administration). But for the institutions that provide health services for those without access to federal aid, budgets will fall by 10% in 2023. In addition, the GOM has asked the House of Representatives for authorization to liquidate during the second quarter of 2023 Fonsabi’s final reserves, which are in a fund that covers tertiary care (high level, specialized care) for the uninsured population. If the reserves are liquidated, it is unclear how this population will access tertiary care.\nThere is little hope for a successful transition in health care systems under the 2023 budget proposal. The proposed joint budget for Insabi and IMSS-Bienestar will be around 127.8 billion pesos, 4% (5.5 billion pesos) less than in 2022 and 1.5% (1.3 billion pesos) less than in the previous administration, on average. The loss is more serious if we just count subsidies for the provision of health services. The proposed subsidies could reach 94.9 billion pesos or 4% (3.8 billion pesos) less than this year and 6.2% (6 billion pesos) less than the previous administration’s average.\nMexico’s human and physical capital in the health sector is inferior to that of other parts of the world. Unfortunately, the future does not look much better. Federal government health spending that truly benefits Mexicans is less than the official figures suggest. This means that, among other things, already poor sanitary conditions in hospitals and clinics will remain or worsen. Limitations in health care funding will limit the early detection of diseases and the prospects for recovery, and increase the cost of treatments. In the case of chronic diseases, late detection and lack of follow-up visits can increase deaths. Mexico’s shortage of vaccines in general will cause a resurgence of preventable diseases, which in turn can trigger epidemics that were already controlled and increase suffering and death. Under current health care conditions in Mexico, there is a greater probability than in previous years of dying from an avoidable cause.\nEducation Overview\nEducation in Mexico is suffering from several crises that will have an important impact on the economy. Studies have found that learning shortages during the pandemic will likely have a negative impact on the affected students’ incomes as adults; they could lose the equivalent of a year’s worth of earnings. In addition, three out of four companies in Mexico are currently struggling to fill their vacancies. It is important to view education policy as one of the keys to solve the crisis to come, but is this what we are seeing in Mexico?\nSeveral events that drew the media’s attention in 2022 will continue to impact Mexico’s education outlook in 2023. Throughout 2022, a current theme in the public debate has been the transition to the “Nueva Escuela Mexicana” (New Mexican School) curriculum. This curriculum takes a community-centered and interdisciplinary approach whereby students learn through a communitarian approach. It differs from the previous curriculum, which focused on an approach centered on students and the knowledge, skills and abilities to function in society. The position of Secretary of Education also has been part of the public discussion, as it is seen as a platform to advance the political careers of those who fill it. Regrettably, other more relevant issues like learning losses caused by the COVID-19 pandemic and significant but unjustified cutbacks to education spending are not getting equal attention.\nAmid this concerning scenario, and the administration’s prioritization of discourse over action, scholars across Mexico have tried to return to the spotlight, with increasing participation in conferences and forums on evidence-based public policy actions. Thus, the 2023 education outlook must be analyzed through two lenses: decisions by government officials that are based on their views of the road ahead and the on-the-ground reality of schools, teachers, and students facing a learning crisis and emotional upheaval caused by the isolation, uncertainty, and stress of school closures during the pandemic.\nGiven the severe impact of the pandemic on learning outcomes around the world, many international studies have estimated the effect of learning losses during school closures in Mexico. Scholars have found that Mexico’s children have lost between 33% to 100% of one school year’s worth of learning. Learning losses were highly unequal. The impact of school closures for students depended on parental educational attainment and economic resources available in the household. In the long run, students could lose the equivalent of up to two years of learning if recovery policies are ineffective.\nDespite a clear education crisis in Mexico, it appears the government’s only plan for 2023 is a pilot program of national strategies that seek to address high drop-out rates, to increase lifelong learning trajectories, to assist in post-pandemic socioemotional recovery, and to offer remedial education to minority groups. The National Commission for the Improvement of Education (or in Spanish, Mejoredu) has also announced a series of publications that profess to meet teacher training needs, with the objective of ensuring that students get a quality education at schools led by well-trained principals and teachers. Toward this end, Mejoredu has offered schools a voluntary standardized assessment so that strategies can be adapted to the needs of every community, and thus improve learning benchmarks.\nIn contrast, state governments—where opposition parties are in place—have developed different assessment strategies to compensate for learning losses. These strategies are expected to be a relevant part of their 2023 education agenda, highlighting a difference between state-level priorities and those of the federal government.\nMexico’s education agenda will be impacted by electoral tensions that continue to grow as the 2024 presidential election nears. This is not because of popular interest in education public policy, but rather, because of the size and power of teacher unions in most Mexican regions; teachers are always a key feature ahead of Mexico’s elections.\nTeacher influence on Mexico’s education policy can be seen in the federal budget: 26% of the federal education budget is allocated for scholarships while 61% goes toward administrative expenses that include principal and teacher salaries.\nIn recent years, tensions have increased between teacher unions and state governments because of delayed or missed salary payments. The federal government is beginning to take advantage of such tensions by saying it will regain control of the teachers' payroll. In 2023, it will be clear if the federal government honors promises to regularize high school teacher contracts in different states and to implement a salary increase. It is unlikely that federal authorities will be able to take over all teacher payrolls in primary and secondary schools, as the federal budget seems insufficient for this to happen. The authorities have already spent most of the extra funds the government had at the beginning of the López Obrador administration (i.e., savings that several previous administrations had accumulated for financial emergencies). As a result, the government has implemented a series of budget cuts—including to several education programs—to finance the president’s main infrastructure programs, such as the Refinery in Dos Bocas, the new airport in Mexico City, and the Mayan Train.\nThe promise of a curriculum change may not be fulfilled in 2023. The educational crisis in schools caused by learning losses and economic grievances might make it impossible to deploy such a change, especially when schools must spend time deciding how to spend and keep resources from \"La Escuela es Nuestra” (the government’s school-based infrastructure and food program). However, this program will likely serve as a lever for the government to maintain presidential approval rates at regional and community levels.\nAn international event that could influence Mexico’s educational outlook is the release of the results from PISA, the OECD’s Program for International Student Assessment. Due to the pandemic and federal government inaction in Mexico, poor results are expected, and could mark 2023 as the year when local recovery strategies become an important part of the solution to the current learning and mental health crisis. In the last couple of years, some schools have organized their own initiatives to help students and teachers. Also, state authorities have developed alliances with the World Bank and Tecnológico de Monterrey, among others, to implement learning assessments and recovery strategies. The results of the assessments at the primary level in Guanajuato, and at the high school level in Nuevo León and Querétaro, helped schools develop specific measures that tackle learning losses and that most likely will have significant, positive results in the years to come.\nVII. Escalating Violence Amid New Opportunities for Organized Crime\nDrug Cartel Disputes Between Sinaloa and CJNG in 2023\nThe conflict between the Sinaloa Cartel and the Cártel de Jalisco Nueva Generación (CJNG) is the defining battle of Mexico’s drug war. Two countervailing possibilities exist for the Sinaloa Cartel moving into 2023. In the first, factions within the Sinaloa Cartel could fragment and move into internal conflict. In the second, which we think is more likely, the Sinaloa Cartel will work diligently to counterbalance the CJNG. The Sinaloa Cartel does not have the luxury of fragmentation between groups such as the Chapitos- and Mayo Zambada-led factions. External pressures thus promote internal cohesion. We will see a continued expansion of the CJNG, limited only by internal fragmentation, and proxy Sinaloa Cartel forces (another driver of violence).\nViolent Hotspots\nWe expect continued violence in the broader Tierra Caliente region comprising the State of Mexico, Michoacan, and Guerrero. We expect port cities such as Manzanillo, Colima, and the surrounding states to be hotly contested directly or by proxy between the Sinaloa Cartel and the CJNG. Ports are critically important to the synthetic drug trade, given the need for chemical precursors imported from China and increasingly, India. We further expect states along the border to see high levels of violence as this battle plays out in states such as Baja California and Sonora. High levels of violence will continue in Zacatecas and Guanajuato where oil pipelines and the roads to the border have made these states more valuable in an era of fentanyl trafficking and oil theft.\nMore High-impact Violence", "excerpt_sha256": "ccd7ce9ef762dae8dbae75d6cd89b5c6254a7f1be66b170764af08b9011acbdf", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E002", "source_id": "SRC-3c3762c63f8d8ebc", "url": "https://sidof.segob.gob.mx/notas/docFuente/5675919", "title": "Reglas de Operación del Programa IMSS-BIENESTAR para el ejercicio fiscal 2023", "publisher": "Diario Oficial de la Federación", "raw_sha256": "90aa8829a729a7dc4dca51c82c22794c4d5d3468f9dabe011b4ee6cfc2d0827b", "fact_summary": "IMSS-BIENESTAR’s rules described preventive, epidemiological, ambulatory, hospital, specialty, palliative and rehabilitation services, associated medicines, community participation and citizen oversight for people without social security. Objetivos\n2.1.\nGeneral\nContribuir al bienestar social e igualdad de las personas, otorgando atención a población sin seguridad\nsocial mediante un MAS-BIENESTAR que vincula los servicios de salud y la Acción Comunitaria.\nEl Programa desarrollará estos objetivos mediante los componentes de: Atención a la Salud y Acción\nComunitaria. Lo anterior, en un marco de respeto a los derechos humanos, con perspectiva de género y con\nEnfoque Intercultural en Salud a la población usuaria, con base en el MAS-BIENESTAR, que, a su vez, se\nsustenta en el Modelo de Atención Integral a la Salud (MAIS).\nCuadro 1.\nModelo de Atención a las Salud para el Bienestar (MAS-BIENESTAR)\n2.2.\nEspecíficos\n2.2.1.\nRealizar las acciones de prevención, vigilancia epidemiológica y salud pública de acuerdo con el\nPrograma Sectorial de Salud 2020-2024 y con el Programa Estratégico de Salud para el Bienestar.\n2.2.2.\nOtorgar servicios de salud ambulatorios y hospitalarios, de especialidad, paliativos y de\nrehabilitación, así como los medicamentos asociados, a la población sin seguridad social, a través de sus\nunidades de salud y hospitales, con Enfoque Intercultural en Salud\n.\n2.2.3.\n\nImplementar estrategias comunitarias para impulsar la participación a nivel individual, familiar y\ncomunitario en acciones de promoción en el autocuidado de la salud; identificación y derivación oportuna a\npersonas con necesidades de atención médica; así como apoyar en la atención a aquellas que requieran los\nservicios de los albergues comunitarios tutelados por el Programa\n.\n2.2.4.\nFomentar la participación ciudadana y la contraloría social, a través del Aval Ciudadano, con la\nfinalidad de dar seguimiento a la calidad en la prestación de los servicios médicos, conocer la satisfacción de\nlos usuarios, sus inconformidades, quejas, sugerencias y felicitaciones, o en su caso, formular propuestas\npara la mejora continua de los establecimientos médicos del Programa.\n3. Lineamientos\n3.1.\nCobertura\nLa distribución por OOAD y nivel de atención de las 4,063 unidades de salud y hospitales actuales del\nPrograma se muestra en el siguiente cuadro.\nCuadro 2. infraestructura en los OOAD por asignación o\ndonación de inmuebles en favor del Programa, o bien, derivado de la baja de unidades de salud o\nmodificaciones en su tipología\n.\nEl número de camas censables (incluidas incubadoras) y no censables con las que cuenta la oferta de\natención mediante internamiento en hospitales del Programa, por considerarse clave en la asignación de\ncapital humano, recursos para medicamentos, insumos, reparaciones, medición regular de la ocupación\nhospitalaria y registro en el sistema de información de la Secretaría de Salud, requiere la autorización de la\nUnidad del Programa.\nAdicionalmente, el Programa despliega estrategias o acciones encaminadas a la promoción de la salud,\ncomplementarias de salud pública o de índole comunitario.\n3.1.1.\n\nUniverso de trabajo\nLocalidades que se ubican en el área geográfica que atiende el Programa y comprende la localidad sede\nde la unidad de salud y las localidades de acción intensiva (LAI).\n3.1.2.\nCobertura por Zona de Servicios Médicos\nLa Zona de Servicios Médicos está conformada por un hospital\ny una o más zonas de supervisión, la cual\nconstituye la unidad funcional para prestar los servicios de salud con el objetivo de vincular la continuidad de\nla atención de los pacientes dentro de una RISS.\nLa Zona de Servicios Médicos tiene un promedio de cobertura poblacional de 150 mil personas. Dicha\ncobertura podrá variar conforme a las condiciones geográficas, la facilidad de acceso a los servicios para la\npoblación, así como a la posible extensión de los servicios de salud por parte del Programa.\nLa cobertura promedio de población a atender de un hospital en su localidad sede va de 15 a 30 mil\npersonas, mientras que para una unidad fija de salud es de aproximadamente 3 mil personas.\nEn el caso de las unidades itinerantes, éstas prestan servicios de salud mediante rutas establecidas a\npoblaciones geográficamente dispersas.\nCorresponde al personal de salud del Programa estabilizar, derivar y orientar a los pacientes y sus familias\npara acceder a servicios de salud de mayor complejidad, cuando se rebase la capacidad resolutiva de la\noferta de servicios que proporciona el Programa.\n3.1.3.\nAmpliación de cobertura\nEl Programa se suma a la política pública del Sector Salud implementada por el Gobierno Federal, para lo\ncual, colaborará de manera articulada con IMSS-BIENESTAR, en la implementación de la estrategia de\nfederalización, conforme a los convenios que éste celebre con las entidades federativas, para que con base\nen el MAS-BIENESTAR se amplíe la cobertura de servicios de salud a la población sin seguridad social\n\ncontribuyendo así a garantizar el acceso universal y gratuito de estos servicios, a fin de coadyuvar en la\nconstrucción del Sistema Único de Salud para el Bienestar.\n3.1.4.\nLocalidad sede\nEs aquella en la que opera una unidad de salud del Programa. Cuando las localidades cuentan con\ninfraestructura de más de una institución de salud, el área geográfica se divide en sectores de responsabilidad\npor acuerdo interinstitucional.\n3.1.5.\nLocalidad de Atención Intensiva (LAI)\nLocalidad aledaña a una unidad de salud del Programa, cuyos habitantes reciben servicios a través de\nalguno de sus componentes, apoyados principalmente por los grupos voluntarios de la organización\ncomunitaria para la salud.\n3.1.6.\nÁrea de influencia\nLocalidades que no pertenecen al universo de trabajo del Programa cuyos habitantes demandan servicios\na través de alguno de sus componentes.\n3.2.\nPoblación\n3.2.1.\nPoblación potencial\nPersonas sin seguridad social en el país.\n3.2.2.\nPoblación objetivo\nPersonas sin seguridad social que residen en localidades del universo de trabajo. Esta población se\nencuentra inscrita en el PPA del Programa.\n\n3.2.3.\nPoblación atendida\nAquella que es beneficiada por alguna de las acciones en salud del Programa y está inscrita en el PPA.\n3.3.\nBeneficiarios\nEs aquella persona que ha recibido algún servicio derivado de las atenciones que proporciona el\nPrograma. Los datos personales de los beneficiarios serán registrados en el PPA.\nLos servicios del Programa están dirigidos a la población sin seguridad social, con el único requisito de\nregistrarse en el PPA mediante la presentación de su CURP o acta de nacimiento e identificación oficial.\n3.3.1.\nPadrón de Población Adscrita\nEl Programa cuenta con un PPA que es una base de información construida a partir del levantamiento de\ninformación de la población, la cual se actualiza de manera permanente en términos de lo dispuesto en el\nprocedimiento normativo institucional; y es sometida a revisiones periódicas de acuerdo con la disponibilidad\npresupuestal del Programa.\nEl\nPPA incorpora una lista nominal de la población que cumple alguna de las siguientes condiciones:\ni) Habita en el universo de trabajo y no cuenta con seguridad social.\nii) Habita en el universo de trabajo, cuenta con acceso a algún sistema de seguridad social y se le han\notorgado servicios a través de algún componente del Programa.\niii) Habita en el área de influencia y sin importar su condición de aseguramiento se le otorga servicio\nrecurrente a través de algún componente del Programa.\niv)\nMigrante extranjero y se le otorga servicio a través de algún componente del Programa durante su\ntránsito por el territorio nacional.", "excerpt_sha256": "13fb13d3821fe4aa4a730f71f6d9813aabec3e308ac0457670259a2e8c2212f4", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt lists 4,075, not merely a count of hospitals and health units independently verified in 2023.", "The text describes intended or authorized arrangements and a proposed federalization strategy, not completed expansion or effective universal coverage."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E003", "source_id": "SRC-3c3762c63f8d8ebc", "url": "https://sidof.segob.gob.mx/notas/docFuente/5675919", "title": "Reglas de Operación del Programa IMSS-BIENESTAR para el ejercicio fiscal 2023", "publisher": "Diario Oficial de la Federación", "raw_sha256": "90aa8829a729a7dc4dca51c82c22794c4d5d3468f9dabe011b4ee6cfc2d0827b", "fact_summary": "The program required preventive and integrated care, patient safety, evidence-based practice, dignity, non-discrimination, continuous-improvement plans, staff training, patient-rights protections and complaint systems. Beneficiaries were entitled to free, equal and confidential care, informed consent, second opinions, emergency attention and responses to complaints through institutional bodies and citizen representatives.", "question_ids": ["3B.7", "6B.2", "6B.3", "6B.5", "6B.8", "8B.6"], "polarity": "favorable", "period_fit": "Directly relevant to the 2023 operating framework.", "ruler_attribution": "These were federal program requirements adopted during López Obrador’s administration.", "limitations": ["The rules do not demonstrate compliance in practice or remedy of violations."], "start_unit": 38, "end_unit": 45, "locator": "extracted HTML block 38 through extracted HTML block 45", "exact_excerpt": "de expresiones\nciudadanas.\n·\nCarteles.\n·\nBuzones.\nEl Programa fundamenta su operación en el MAS-BIENESTAR, y sus características son:\na)\nPromueve la Atención Primaria a la Salud como puerta de entrada al componente de salud y de\nacciones comunitarias del Programa.\nb)\nProporciona atención a la salud de manera integral y oportuna, en los diferentes niveles de atención,\nprivilegiando el enfoque preventivo a los principales daños a la salud.\nc)\nParte de un diagnóstico de salud local que analiza y jerarquiza los riesgos y daños más frecuentes a\nla salud\n,\npara que, con apoyo del sistema de vigilancia epidemiológica, se realicen acciones de\nprevención y educación para la salud, en conjunto con la Organización Comunitaria y la población.\nd)\nPropicia el aprovechamiento de los conocimientos y recursos de salud disponibles, incluidos los de la\nmedicina tradicional.\ne)\nBasa sus intervenciones en salud en un sistema integral de calidad que se enfoca a la seguridad de\nlos pacientes, el trato digno y exigibilidad de los derechos de los pacientes, planes de mejora\n\ncontinua y socialización de las experiencias exitosas en calidad.\nf)\nPromueve la participación individual, familiar y colectiva en las acciones de salud, prácticas\nsaludables, saneamiento básico, promoción de entornos saludables y del rescate de alimentos\nlocales a través de la Organización Comunitaria para la Salud.\ng)\nPersigue la eficiencia y la efectividad, mediante un sistema de información, desempeño, seguimiento,\nsupervisión y evaluación, basado en metas para los diferentes niveles del Programa, que busca la\nadecuada gestión de los procesos, las ganancias en salud, las mejoras de la calidad de vida y las\nacciones de mejora continua.\nh)\nEstablece alianzas estratégicas con otras instituciones de salud y de asistencia social, con órdenes\nde gobierno municipal, estatal y federal; así como con la iniciativa social y privada para optimizar\nrecursos.\ni)\nPromueve la contraloría social y la participación ciudadana, mediante la medición de la satisfacción,\nla promoción de canales de comunicación y la atención a las expresiones ciudadanas, por áreas de\ncompetencia.\nj)\nFacilita una atención integral a la salud mediante el irrestricto respeto a los derechos humanos,\nIgualdad de Género y la no discriminación con Enfoque Intercultural en Salud.\nLos servicios y medicamentos que otorga el Programa, en los diferentes niveles de atención, tendrán\ncomo referencia el Compendio Nacional de Insumos para la Salud, la nomenclatura, descripción,\nmedicamentos asociados y clasificación diagnóstica (CIE-10).\nLa Unidad del Programa aprobará anualmente, el listado de Medicamentos y Material de Curación\n\nreferidos en el Compendio Nacional de Insumos para la Salud, que se aplicará en las unidades de salud y\nhospitales del Programa, considerando los niveles y modalidades de atención, así también los criterios de\nefectividad, seguridad y costo, de acuerdo a los protocolos de atención.\nPara el caso de vacunas y otros productos biológicos, se atenderán las recomendaciones de inclusión y\nuso establecidas por el Consejo Nacional de Vacunación (CONAVA) y su adquisición se realizará ajustándose\na la descripción establecida en el Compendio Nacional de Insumos para la Salud.\n3.5.\nCalidad de la atención y derechos de los beneficiarios\n3.5.1.\nCalidad de la atención\nEn las unidades de salud y hospitales del Programa, además del respeto y la promoción de los derechos\nde los pacientes, se cuenta con un sistema de calidad integral que garantiza un trato digno, atención basada\nen la evidencia científica, protección a la seguridad de los pacientes y la escucha de los usuarios y sus\nfamilias. Dicho sistema se articula a través de la Coordinación de Educación y Calidad; y el COCASEP.\nPara garantizar la calidad del Componente de Atención a la Salud del Programa, la práctica de los\nprofesionales en las unidades de salud y hospitales bajo su responsabilidad estará basada en las Normas\nOficiales Mexicanas aplicables, así como los protocolos de atención que emita la SSA.\nAtendiendo a las posibilidades presupuestarias y de organización del Programa, se dará continuidad con\nla\nestrategia destinada a la certificación gradual de las unidades de salud y hospitales del Programa, ante el\nConsejo de Salubridad General.\nCon el fin de proteger la seguridad de los pacientes atendidos en las unidades de salud y hospitales del\nPrograma, se desarrollarán programas de capacitación en cultura de la seguridad de los pacientes y serán\nobjeto de seguimiento las Metas Internacionales de Seguridad del Paciente, en los diferentes niveles de\natención en términos de lo dispuesto en el Acuerdo por el que se declara la obligatoriedad de la\n\nimplementación, para todos los integrantes del Sistema Nacional de Salud, del documento denominado\nAcciones Esenciales para la Seguridad del Paciente, publicado en el DOF el 08 de septiembre de 2017.\nLos expedientes clínicos de los pacientes atendidos en las unidades de salud y hospitales del Programa\nse ajustarán a lo establecido en la Norma Oficial Mexicana.\nNOM-004-SSA3-2012, del Expediente Clínico. Las unidades de salud velarán por la calidad de los\nmismos, y se aplicará de manera regular el MECIC y realizarán el archivo y custodia de los expedientes en la\nforma reglamentariamente establecida.\nPara promover el uso racional de medicamentos, el Programa desarrollará temas de capacitación para los\nprofesionales de la salud en relación a transcripción, prescripción, preparación, administración de\nmedicamentos y almacenamiento; adicionalmente en esta materia, se informará a los pacientes, familiares o\nacompañantes sobre las indicaciones, posología, forma de administración y contraindicaciones, si las hubiere,\nde los medicamentos prescritos, así como los riesgos inherentes a su manejo y uso inadecuado. Además, se\nrealizarán acciones para el uso adecuado de antibióticos, contribuyendo a la disminución de la resistencia\nantimicrobiana como lo señala la normatividad a través del Comité de Farmacia y Terapéutica.\nEl Programa\nrealizará anualmente y a partir de los recursos presupuestales disponibles, estudios de\nsatisfacción de sus beneficiarios con los servicios recibidos,\ncon el propósito de incidir en la mejora continua\nde la calidad de los servicios.\n3.5.2.\nCapacitación\nLa capacitación que se realiza en el Programa se planea en el nivel central con base en el panorama\nepidemiológico, prioridades en salud y necesidades del personal operativo y directivo.\nPara mejorar la atención de la calidad médica, la Unidad del Programa gestiona y coordina la actualización\ndel personal médico y paramédico en los niveles de conducción y operativos, considerando los perfiles del\n\npuesto.\nEn el nivel del OOAD la Jefatura de Servicios de Prestaciones Médicas, el EGAS, EMS, Cuerpos de\nGobierno y ESAZ facilitan los cursos de inducción al puesto a los integrantes de nuevo ingreso de los equipos\nde conducción, así como personal operativo y becario.\nEn las reuniones zonales se da prioridad a la capacitación en las cuales participa personal médico, de\nenfermería y promotor de Acción Comunitaria.\nEn los hospitales a través del Coordinador de Educación y Calidad se establece un programa de\ncapacitación anual que involucra a las diferentes disciplinas de los diferentes turnos.\nEn forma permanente cada uno de los equipos de conducción del OOAD da seguimiento a la capacitación\nproporcionada a personal institucional y becario durante las visitas de supervisión-asesoría.\nLos equipos de salud del primer y segundo nivel de atención en coordinación con el promotor de Acción\nComunitaria proporcionan orientación a los grupos voluntarios de la estructura comunitaria para garantizar el\nMAS-BIENESTAR, en cumplimiento con el programa de capacitación a grupos voluntarios.\n3.5.3.\nSupervisión, asesoría y seguimiento\nEl Programa establecerá desde nivel central el mecanismo de supervisión, asesoría y seguimiento,\nestableciendo compromisos y acuerdos con base en resultados, a los cuales dará seguimiento.\nA su vez el personal de conducción del Programa en los OOAD, serán los responsables de realizar dicho\nproceso en su ámbito de responsabilidad, estableciendo compromisos y acuerdos a los cuales dará\nseguimiento.\nEsta supervisión, asesoría y seguimiento, deberá tener carácter multidisciplinario, optimizando tiempo y\nrecursos, colaborará en el cumplimiento de los objetivos, indicadores y metas, con el propósito de facilitar la\ntoma de decisiones, detectar oportunamente desviaciones operativas o de conducción y adoptar, en su caso,\nmedidas correctivas e implementar acciones de mejora continua, a efecto de dar seguimiento y ponderar los\navances en relación con los objetivos planteados.\n\nLas acciones de supervisión, asesoría y seguimiento, que en su caso se desarrollen por el Programa,\nadoptarán obligatoriamente las siguientes modalidades:\nSeguimiento regular de los procesos en los que se estructuran los componentes del Programa, a través de\nmedios escritos y/o electrónicos, videoconferencias, solicitudes de información y visitas, para brindar asesoría,\ncomprobar, instruir y apoyar el desarrollo y ejecución de las prioridades en salud, así como valorar su grado\nde avance y el cumplimiento de los objetivos de estas ROP, los indicadores y las metas establecidas por la\nUnidad del Programa, así como la ejecución del MAS-BIENESTAR.\nPlan de Supervisión, Asesoría y Seguimiento del Nivel Central del Programa,\nlos integrantes de los EGAS,\nEMS, Cuerpos de Gobierno y ESAZ realizarán visitas programadas de supervisión y asesoría integral, de\nmanera multidisciplinaria, a las unidades de salud de los diferentes niveles de atención a su cargo, incluyendo\nprocesos acordes a las prioridades, estrategias, componentes del Programa y los aspectos técnico médico\nadministrativos. Esta supervisión se realizará mediante la aplicación de cédulas de supervisión elaboradas por\nla Unidad del Programa, las cuales incluyen el desempeño directivo y evaluación de la calidad de la atención,\nse elaborarán compromisos específicos acordes a las cédulas de supervisión y normatividad, con registro en\nlas bitácoras de cada unidad de salud de primer nivel supervisada; para el caso de los hospitales se debe\nutilizar el formato de compromisos establecido por la Unidad del Programa\n.\nPlan de Supervisión, Asesoría y Seguimiento del Nivel Operativo del Programa,\nen relación con el cual los\nintegrantes de los EGAS, EMS, Cuerpos de Gobierno y ESAZ realizarán visitas de supervisión y asesoría\nintegral, de manera multidisciplinaria, a las unidades de salud de los diferentes niveles de atención a su cargo,\nincluyendo procesos acordes a las prioridades, estrategias, componentes del Programa y los aspectos técnico\nmédico administrativos. Esta supervisión se realizará mediante la aplicación de cédulas de supervisión que\nincluye desempeño directivo y evaluación de la calidad de la atención, se elaborarán compromisos específicos\n\ncon registro en las bitácoras de cada unidad de salud de primer nivel supervisada; para el caso de los\nhospitales se debe utilizar el formato de compromisos establecido por la Unidad.\nLos EGAS deben supervisar un hospital y dos unidades de salud de primer nivel por mes. El EMS debe\nvisitar un hospital y al menos cinco unidades de salud de primer nivel por mes.\nLos Cuerpos de Gobierno de Hospital deben programar visitas conjuntas al 100% de las áreas y servicios,\ndurante los bimestres febrero-marzo y julio-agosto, independientemente de la supervisión individual. El ESAZ\ndebe supervisar el 100% de las unidades de salud bajo su responsabilidad al menos cada dos meses.\nNo se considerarán como visitas de supervisión la aplicación parcial de las cédulas de supervisión, sin\nbase normativa y/o sin la participación integral multidisciplinaria de acuerdo con el nivel de conducción.\n3.5.4.\nDerechos de los beneficiarios\nLos beneficiarios del Programa tendrán derecho en los establecimientos médicos a:\n·\nRecibir atención médica gratuita, con igualdad y sin discriminación.\n·\nRecibir un trato digno y respetuoso.\n·\nRecibir información sobre su proceso de atención, suficiente, clara, oportuna y veraz.\n·\nDecidir libremente sobre su atención.\n·\nOtorgar o no su consentimiento válidamente informado.\n·\nSer tratado con confidencialidad.\n·\n\nContar con facilidades para obtener una segunda opinión.\n·\nRecibir atención médica en caso de urgencia.\n·\nContar con un Expediente Clínico.\n·\nSer atendido cuando se inconforme con los servicios recibidos.\n·\nRecibir atención médica libre de riesgos mediante procedimientos seguros.\nPara hacer efectivos estos derechos, las unidades de salud y hospitales del Programa contarán con un\nSistema de Atención Ciudadana, para captar las quejas, sugerencias y felicitaciones, así como con\nprocedimientos que incluyan su análisis regular por los Equipos de Conducción y el COCASEP, con la\nparticipación del Aval Ciudadano, y siendo turnadas y atendidas por las distintas áreas en el ámbito de sus\nresponsabilidades y competencias.\n3.6.\nInstancias normativas y participantes\nLa operación del Programa está regulada por la legislación federal aplicable, las presentes Reglas de\nOperación, Normas Oficiales Mexicanas (NOM), concernientes a la Cartera de Servicios del Programa,\nProtocolos de Atención emitidos por la Secretaría de Salud, documentos normativos del IMSS que le resulten\naplicables, Procedimientos, Guías, Lineamientos y oficios circulares emitidos por la Unidad del Programa.\n3.6.1.\nInstancia normativa\nCorresponde a la Unidad del Programa la definición de los aspectos que regulan su ejecución. visitas, para brindar asesoría,\ncomprobar, instruir y apoyar el desarrollo y ejecución de las prioridades en salud, así como valorar su grado\nde avance y el cumplimiento de los objetivos de estas Esta supervisión se realizará mediante la aplicación de cédulas de supervisión que\nincluye desempeño directivo y evaluación de la calidad de la atención, se elaborarán compromisos específicos\n\ncon registro en las bitácoras de cada unidad de salud de primer nivel supervisada; para el caso de los\nhospitales se debe utilizar el formato de compromisos establecido por la Unidad.\nLos EGAS deben supervisar un hospital y dos unidades de salud de primer nivel por mes. El EMS debe\nvisitar un hospital y al menos cinco unidades de salud de primer nivel por mes.\nLos Cuerpos de Gobierno de Hospital deben programar visitas conjuntas al 100% de las áreas y servicios,\ndurante los bimestres febrero-marzo y julio-agosto, independientemente de la supervisión individual. El ESAZ\ndebe supervisar el 100% de las unidades de salud bajo su responsabilidad al menos cada dos meses.\nNo se considerarán como visitas de supervisión la aplicación parcial de las cédulas de supervisión, sin\nbase normativa y/o sin la participación integral multidisciplinaria de acuerdo con el nivel de conducción.\n3.5.4.\nDerechos de los beneficiarios\nLos beneficiarios del Programa tendrán derecho en los establecimientos médicos a:\n·\nRecibir atención médica gratuita, con igualdad y sin discriminación.\n·\nRecibir un trato digno y respetuoso.\n·\nRecibir información sobre su proceso de atención, suficiente, clara, oportuna y veraz.\n·\nDecidir libremente sobre su atención.\n·\nOtorgar o no su consentimiento válidamente informado.\n·\nSer tratado con confidencialidad.\n·\n\nContar con facilidades para obtener una segunda opinión.\n·\nRecibir atención médica en caso de urgencia.\n·\nContar con un Expediente Clínico.\n·\nSer atendido cuando se inconforme con los servicios recibidos.\n·\nRecibir atención médica libre de riesgos mediante procedimientos seguros.\nPara hacer efectivos estos derechos, las unidades de salud y hospitales del Programa contarán con un\nSistema de Atención Ciudadana, para captar las quejas, sugerencias y felicitaciones, así como con\nprocedimientos que incluyan su análisis regular por los Equipos de Conducción y el COCASEP, con la\nparticipación del Aval Ciudadano, y siendo turnadas y atendidas por las distintas áreas en el ámbito de sus\nresponsabilidades y competencias.\n3.6.\nInstancias normativas y participantes\nLa operación del Programa está regulada por la legislación federal aplicable, las presentes Reglas de\nOperación, Normas Oficiales Mexicanas (NOM), concernientes a la Cartera de Servicios del Programa,\nProtocolos de Atención emitidos por la Secretaría de Salud, documentos normativos del IMSS que le resulten\naplicables, Procedimientos, Guías, Lineamientos y oficios circulares emitidos por la Unidad del Programa.\n3.6.1.\nInstancia normativa\nCorresponde a la Unidad del Programa la definición de los aspectos que regulan su ejecución. Tendrá las\nsiguientes facultades:\nI.\n\nOrganizar la administración y operación de los dos componentes del Programa, en sus diferentes\nniveles de conducción y operación, en colaboración articulada con IMSS-BIENESTAR, atendiendo\na los objetivos, alineados al Programa Estratégico de Salud para el Bienestar y en términos de los\nConvenios de Colaboración que, para tal efecto, suscriba el IMSS con It described increased military influence despite demilitarization rhetoric, centralization of power and weakening of independent checks, an insufficient nearshoring strategy, deteriorating security, personal austerity and persistent anti-corruption rhetoric. It also credited restrained pandemic spending with avoiding a significant fiscal crisis and credited him with successfully negotiating the USMCA.", "question_ids": ["2B.10", "3B.10", "4B.10", "4B.4", "5B.1", "5B.10", "5B.3", "8B.10", "8B.7"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Published July 2023 and directly evaluates the administration before the 2024 transition.", "ruler_attribution": "The assessment concerns López Obrador’s presidency and policies.", "limitations": ["The report is an analytical assessment rather than an official audit; some claims rely on cited secondary sources."], "start_unit": 5, "end_unit": 7, "locator": "page 5 through page 7", "exact_excerpt": "Introduction\nPresident Andrés Manuel López Obrador, known widely by the initials AMLO, aimed to transform\nthe politics, economy, and security of Mexico. He calls his wide-ranging series of reforms the\n“fourth transformation,” placing it on par with other great moments in Mexican history: Mexico’s\nindependence, the Mexican Revolution, and the founding of the modern Mexican state. AMLO is nothing if\nnot ambitious, envisaging himself in the same echelon as Mexico’s national heroes Benito Juárez, Porfirio Díaz,\nand—AMLO’s personal favorite—Lazaro Cárdenas. But as his sexenio—Mexico’s six-year presidential term—ends\nand AMLO has no possibility for reelection, it is time to take stock of his legacy. A full accounting of what\nMexico has experienced and what kind of Mexico AMLO will leave his successor is in dire order. What will\nMexico look like in 2024?\nAt times, AMLO appears to be bulletproof, though his policies, even through a favorable lens, have had\nmixed results at best. At worst, they have proved counterproductive and even hypocritical. He speaks\nof environmental stewardship while pouring money into the oil industry and overseeing an uptick in\ndeforestation. He proclaims a demilitarized approach to security while expanding the military’s influence over\ndaily life and wielding a new national guard gendarmerie indiscriminately. He harkens to antiestablishment\nand populist credentials while centralizing power and defanging independent institutions meant to check the\nexecutive. Indeed, by a staggering array of metrics, from citizen security to rural development to democratic\naccountability, AMLO’s policies actively work against his constituents and self-professed policy objectives.\nNevertheless, AMLO’s personal austerity and a persistent rhetoric of anti-corruption mean his approval ratings\nremain among the highest in Latin America. Even after an unprecedented 8 percent drop in approval following\nhis attack on the popular National Electoral Institute (INE), his approval continued to hover at 60 percent.1\nAMLO’s policies have, in turn, influenced Mexico’s economic, security, and political context with uneven and, at\ntimes, paradoxical results. Mexico is inattentive to and seemingly uninterested in nearshoring, lacking a defined\n1 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nstrategy for capitalizing on shifting global supply chains and embroiled in a set of unproductive trade disputes\nwith its North American neighbors. At the same time, the nation is home to a dynamic and vibrant economy,\nwhich has seen growth in key sectors, from traditional manufacturing to emerging digital technologies and\nsemiconductors. In terms of security, the forecast is bleak, with efforts to curb organized crime deteriorating\nto the point of spurring harmful proposals from the U.S. Congress to authorize the use of military force\nagainst Mexico’s cartels. Nevertheless, a glimmer of potential remains, as long-standing linkages between U.S.\nand Mexican security forces continue to encourage cooperation in the form of the U.S.-Mexico Bicentennial\nFramework for Security, Public Health and Safe Communities.2 Politically, AMLO’s influence has proved corrosive\nto Mexican democracy, presiding over the country’s decline in a few short years from “flawed democracy” to\n“hybrid regime,” according to the Economist Intelligence Unit.3 However, the opposition AMLO has encountered\nto some of his most deleterious political projects suggests a civil society that remains fiercely dedicated to\nprotecting its hard-won democracy, along with nontrivial checks from both the legislative and judicial branches.\nWith fewer than two years remaining in his six-year term, a retrospective is warranted to assess the results\nof the AMLO years, what is yet to come, and what trends will persist long after López Obrador has exited the\nPalacio Nacional. The objective of such an analysis is not to make predictions about who will succeed AMLO\nbut rather to adumbrate the contours of a post-AMLO Mexico in three important dimensions: economy,\nsecurity, and politics.\nIn short, Mexico in 2024 looks economically stagnant despite the once-in-a-generation opportunity presented\nby nearshoring. The nation is weak and debilitated in its fight against ruthless and expanding cartels and\nlimping more than sprinting as democratic institutions continue to suffer debilitating attacks. Overall, the\nMexico of 2024 appears far more battered and bruised than the Mexico AMLO took the reins of in 2018.\n2 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nEconomy\nPresident López Obrador’s economic policies have been marked by significant variation. On the\none hand, his domestic policy has revolved around a series of social programs that suggest the\ngovernment’s improved commitment to providing a social safety net. On the other, during the\npandemic, Mexico was austere in its spending, devoting a fraction of what other large economies in the region\ncommitted to economic stimulus and judiciously avoiding fiscal crises from increased public debt.4 AMLO\nhas derided “neoliberalism” in all its forms, using this critique as a capacious tent under which to move to\ngreater nationalization of industries and revitalization of often sclerotic state-run enterprises. AMLO has also\nweaponized austerity to dismantle institutions and agencies he does not like.5\nTo his credit, he successfully negotiated the U.S.-Mexico-Canada Agreement (USMCA) with the administration\nunder U.S. president Donald Trump, helping to create one of the world’s most modern trade frameworks.\nFurther, Mexico remains a powerhouse as the second-largest economy in Latin America, with promise in both\ntraditional and emerging sectors. However, according to the World Bank’s Global Economic Prospects report,\nMexico’s economy is projected to grow just 0.9 percent in 2023, the lowest rate of growth in all Latin America.6\nAs Mexico continues on the long path to post-pandemic recovery, it has become increasingly apparent that\nmuch of the country’s economic growth is occurring, as one of the authors notes, “in spite of Mexico’s\ngovernment’s policies, rather than because of them.”7\nUltimately, restoration of confidence from investors and the business community will influence Mexico’s\neconomic position after AMLO according to four main themes: the status of energy reform, Mexico’s ability\nto take advantage of the nearshoring moment, the success of AMLO in delivering on promises to develop the\ncountry’s southern and rural areas, and Mexico’s future bureaucratic and regulatory composition.\n3 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "excerpt_sha256": "20e89c343754c2fab849aa026a82735543e0b3f2d8156a60325b45c9db1f44f6", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt does not explicitly support the broader claim that security deterioration was caused by the administration, beyond its analytical assessment of policy effects."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E014", "source_id": "SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1", "url": "https://csis-website-prod.s3.amazonaws.com/s3fs-public/2023-07/230718_Berg_After_AMLO_0.pdf", "title": "After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "publisher": "Center for Strategic and International Studies", "raw_sha256": "112c3fb2368e464f70403bf4f07af82fb3b49ec04385a56a31a037931a4b98bd", "fact_summary": "López Obrador halted new foreign oil contracts, audited more than 100 previous awards for corruption and committed billions to the Dos Bocas refinery, but the refinery was reported substantially over budget. His proposed electricity reform would have strengthened state control and subordinated autonomous regulators; Congress failed to approve it, limiting his ability to enact the full agenda. Lithium nationalization and hostility to foreign energy investment raised concerns about investor confidence and clean-energy transition.", "question_ids": ["5B.1", "5B.10", "5B.3", "5B.4", "5B.5", "5B.6", "8B.10", "8B.2", "8B.6", "8B.7", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The assessment covers policies and legislative outcomes through 2023.", "ruler_attribution": "The decisions and legislative initiative were attributed to López Obrador’s administration.", "limitations": ["The report includes forecasts and estimates as well as completed policy actions."], "start_unit": 8, "end_unit": 9, "locator": "page 8 through page 9", "exact_excerpt": "Energy Reform\nPresident López Obrador undoubtedly has a keen appreciation for the centrality of energy to Mexican politics\nand civil society. Indeed, he was carried into power in no small part by the outpouring of popular discontent\nagainst the Peña Nieto administration in 2017 as protests erupted against rising gas prices. The spike in\ngasoline prices, known as a gasolinazo, was the sharpest rise in Mexico in two decades.8 Overall, prices in\nsome areas rose by 48 percent as the government under President Enrique Peña Nieto passed a major energy\nreform that ended the legal monopoly of Petróleos Mexicanos (Pemex), Mexico’s state-owned oil company.\nPemex holds the dubious title of the world’s most indebted state-run oil firm and is widely reported to lack\nthe funds or technology to fully develop Mexico’s oil reserves (the country ranks 21st in the world in terms of\ncrude oil resources).9 Nevertheless, the dramatic effect of the gasolinazo protests served to reinforce lessons\nthat López Obrador, a long-standing opponent of privatization, had already taken to heart: the energy reforms\nof his predecessor must be rolled back, and gas prices must remain stable.\nAMLO quickly set about working to realize these goals, placing a hold on new contracts for foreign oil\ncompanies and auditing over 100 of the awards signed under Peña Nieto for corruption.10 At the same time,\nin the early months of his presidency, AMLO declared an $8 billion investment for a new Pemex refinery to be\nlocated in his home state of Tabasco, a commitment later supplemented with a $600 million investment from\nChinese state banks.11 Expected to become operational in 2023, the refinery project has already run some $3\nbillion over budget.12\nView of the Mexican oil company PEMEX’s oil complex in Tula, Hidalgo state, Mexico, on January 11, 2018.\nPhoto Source: Pedro Pardo/AFP/Getty Images\nBy far the most ambitious project the president has undertaken is a proposed constitutional reform that would\nsignificantly strengthen the state’s role in the energy sector. Under the proposed law, Mexico’s state-owned\npower company, the Comisión Federal de Electricidad (CFE), would have a guaranteed 54 percent market\nshare while limiting the participation of private electricity suppliers. It would also fold the autonomous Energy\nRegulatory Commission (CRE) and the National Hydrocarbons Commission (CNH) into the federal government\nby making them subordinate to the Ministry of Energy.13\n4 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nThe amendment proved a rallying cry for domestic opposition, which cited inefficiencies within the CFE that,\nby some estimates, would increase operating costs by as much as $3 billion a year while increasing the risk\nof power outages by as much as 30 percent.14 The amendment also sparked major concerns internationally,\nespecially among U.S. energy firms, which voiced fears about the stability of their previous investments.\nUltimately, AMLO was unable to muster the political capital to move the initiative forward, as the lower house\nof Congress failed to reach the two-thirds supermajority needed to advance a constitutional amendment.15 The\nfailure of the reform showcased hard limits on AMLO’s power to drive sweeping legislative change in critical\neconomic sectors, providing a greater degree of clarity for prospective investors and businesses.\nHowever, the defeat of the energy reform amendment was far from an unmitigated victory for opponents. In\nApril 2022, Mexico’s Supreme Court issued an ambiguous decision on an earlier energy law that gave the CFE\npriority access to send its energy to the grid before foreign energy producers.16 Ultimately, AMLO’s handling of\nenergy policy impelled the United States and Canada to seek joint consultations under the USMCA in July 2022.\nThus far, the question of energy policy has proved a thorny issue, with the specter of a dispute resolution\npanel raising lingering questions for Mexico’s domestic industry and external investors alike. It also threatens\nto stall nearshoring momentum.\nHigh energy prices coupled with the CFE’s heavy dependency on oil for power generation mean companies\nstruggle to obtain cost-effective power and meet their environmental, social, and corporate governance (ESG)\ntargets while investing in Mexico. Furthermore, in the wake of the amendment’s defeat, AMLO appears to have\nshifted his attention toward the nationalization of other industries. On April 17, the same day as the vote in the\nlower house, the president introduced a reform to the mining law to nationalize Mexico’s lithium industry. The\nlaw was fast-tracked through Congress and approved just two days later.17\nTHE ENERGY OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nThe nationalization of lithium will likely set Mexico back in the transition to lower carbon emissions and the\npush for climate-smart energy solutions. When coupled with AMLO’s hostility to foreign energy companies,\nmany of which seek to invest in renewable power sources, Mexico has locked itself into a traditional carbon-\nand-oil energy strategy for the near future. Indeed, Pemex is a mainstay of AMLO’s fourth transformation\ndomestic project. Mexico’s ability to diversify this strategy in 2024 will depend, in part, on the extent to which\ninvestor confidence can be restored by resolving Mexico’s energy uncertainty through USMCA arbitration and\nthe willingness of AMLO to strenuously oppose future foreign investments in the energy sector.18 Not only does\nthis policy impair Mexico’s ability to meet clean energy and environmental goals, it also reverses one of the most\npositive trends to come out of the 2013 reform, which prompted a windfall of investment in renewable energy\nindustries.19 Lithium may buck this trend but only if the national lithium company proves capable of rapidly and\nefficiently scaling up production, an unlikely prospect as only one lithium mining project, the Chinese-owned\nBacanora Lithium, appears to stand a realistic chance of beginning production by the end of 2023.20\nAnother key concern in the near term is gasoline price stability at a time of rising global oil prices. While\nAMLO will likely do everything in his power to avoid a gasolinazo under his administration, his energy policies\nwill become increasingly untenable over the medium and long term as the administration pours more funds\ninto subsidized fuel. A deterioration of dispute resolution procedures under the USMCA will only exacerbate\nsuch a downward trend, potentially exposing Mexico to punitive tariffs and stifling necessary investment\nin sectors needed for the energy transition. Thus, AMLO may successfully stave off an energy crisis under\nhis administration, but absent a significant course correction when a future crisis hits, it may produce even\ngreater disruptions than those observed in 2017.\n5 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "excerpt_sha256": "1102c7f963b73f9548d67bfe409b9ce0ad3b90c4c2f60d0df073ad6167accac3", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0051-E017", "source_id": "SRC-cb7adb2d19e644c1", "url": "https://csis-website-prod.s3.amazonaws.com/s3fs-public/2023-07/230718_Berg_After_AMLO_0.pdf", "title": "After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024", "publisher": "Center for Strategic and International Studies", "raw_sha256": "112c3fb2368e464f70403bf4f07af82fb3b49ec04385a56a31a037931a4b98bd", "fact_summary": "CSIS reported about 34,000 homicides in 2021, described 2022 as the most violent year recorded by the National Public Security System, reported 100 political assassinations during the 2021 midterm elections, and stated that criminal organizations had expanded control to claim up to 40 percent of Mexican territory. Each year, Mexico experiences around\n30,000 murders, which have risen to record levels during AMLO’s mandate.60 In 2021 the nation\nregistered 34,000 homicides, double the number in 2015, while 2022 was the most violent year ever recorded\nby the National Public Security System.61 According to Bloomberg, Mexico remains home to eight of the world’s\nmost dangerous cities.62 Insecurity also characterizes the political scene: in the 2021 midterm elections, 100\npolitical assassinations were reported, 35 of which targeted political candidates.63 With a year and a half\nremaining in AMLO’s term, homicides on his watch have already equaled those under his predecessor’s\nsix-year term and have surpassed by 22 percent the total during the six-year term of Felipe Calderón, the\npresident whose war on drugs AMLO has derided. Simply put, AMLO’s sexenio will be the bloodiest six-year\nterm in Mexican history.64\nViolence associated with conflict against drug cartels claims most of these victims. López Obrador’s “abrazos,\nno balazos” (hugs, not bullets) security policy has seen criminal organizations expand their control to claim up\nto 40 percent of Mexican territory.65 With Mexico experiencing some of the highest levels of extortion, human\ntrafficking, human rights violations, and femicides registered in the country’s history, Mexico’s situation under\nAMLO displays an alarming trend.66 These patterns present no signs of slowing, as AMLO’s security reforms\nhave reduced public spending on security, muddied the citizen security landscape, and reduced cooperation\nwith the United States on security and counternarcotics issues.67\n13 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nMembers of the Mexican army drive on a road a day after an operation to arrest the son of Joaquín “El Chapo”\nGuzmán, Ovidio Guzmán, in Culiacán, Sinaloa state, Mexico, on January 6, 2023.\nPhoto Source: Juan Carlos Cruz/AFP/Getty Imges\nOrganized Crime\nAmong the many challenges Mexico faces, organized crime remains one of the most complex and resilient\nthreats the country has yet to find a successful strategy for addressing. In 2015 there were around 25 cartels\nrecognized as major criminal groupings—or what the U.S. Department of Justice calls “well-articulated”\ncriminal groups. By 2019 this figure had grown to 37, which is likely a significant underestimate.68 In fact,\nindependent observers identified the presence of up to 463 criminal entities on Mexican territory, explaining\nthat criminal groupings have grown ever smaller following internal fragmentation and the intensification\nof violence as a result of the government’s targeting of kingpins.69 At the same time, an underworld\nso fragmented and difficult to trace makes it even harder for political leadership to find adequate and\neffective measures to counter its activity, resulting in a vicious cycle. Some of these groups, such as Jalisco\nNew Generation Cartel (CJNG) or the Sinaloa Cartel, are powerful enough to challenge the state, and the\ntransformation and fragmentation they provoke either within or between each other often causes deadly\nepisodes of violence.70 Indeed, U.S. Drug Enforcement Administration (DEA) administrator Anne Milgram has\nstated that these two groups pose “the greatest criminal threat” ever faced by the United States.71\nDuring the pandemic, the role of criminal groups became twofold. On the one hand, they exacerbated insecurity\nas violent attacks on civilians and battles with other groups continued apace. On the other, criminal groups\ncomplemented, or in some cases took on, the role of the state by distributing food and aid and imposing a curfew\nto limit the spread of Covid-19. This was the case for the Sinaloa Cartel, which distributed “Chapo dispensas,”\nor boxes of supplies with erstwhile cartel leader Joaquín “El Chapo” Guzmán’s face emblazoned on them.72\nGovernment restrictions on mobility and tighter border controls also affected their activity, and as a result new\nchannels of criminality arose, such as cybercrimes, which are harder for authorities to detect.73 High profits\nand low risks for activities such as fraud, piracy, and cybertheft quickly attracted drug cartels and generated\n14 | Ryan C.
