At the same time, disputes over energy and genetically modified corn, limits on DEA cooperation, strained security relations and ideological disagreements created uncertainty for trade, investment and counternarcotics cooperation.", "question_ids": ["2B.10", "2B.5", "2B.7", "5B.10", "5B.4", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The report includes the 2023 North American Leaders’ Summit and ongoing 2023 disputes.", "ruler_attribution": "Foreign-policy and cooperation choices were attributed to López Obrador’s government, while bilateral outcomes reflected both governments.", "limitations": ["The source focuses on outlook and policy implications rather than a complete record of executed diplomatic decisions."], "start_unit": 29, "end_unit": 34, "locator": "page 29 through page 34", "exact_excerpt": "several other initiatives, as vital to national security.132 This incident suggests AMLO will not sit idly by in the\nevent his electoral reforms flounder in the courts, and he may attempt a similar maneuver to keep some or all\nof Plan B in place.\nElections and Party Dynamics\nAMLO’s populist, antiestablishment rhetoric helped lead the Morena party to victory in 2018. For the first\ntime, PRI, PAN, and the Party of the Democratic Revolution (PRD) combined obtained only 40 percent of the\nvote, compared to their previous 90 percent share.133 Morena has benefited from incredible popular support\nsince, though signs of decline began to emerge in the 2021 midterm elections.\nThe midterm elections decreased Morena’s supermajority to an absolute majority, which was interpreted by\nmany political analysts as positive for Mexico.134 In fact, the elections were marked by high attendance at polls\nand absence of fraud and violence. More than half of the population voted against Morena, suggesting the desire\nfor more options and willingness to support democracy.135 Although Morena won most gubernatorial races, the\nopposition made significant gains in the Chamber of Deputies.136 This came as a wake-up call for the opposition,\nrevealing its potential to represent an alternative to Morena if unified in a coalition. Even if Morena won more\nrepresentatives, it lost in bigger cities, which might indicate a decrease in support within the middle-class electorate\nit used to appeal to. Following AMLO’s repression of feminist protests, the women’s electorate also turned on him,\nanother sign of emerging disillusion with Morena that could have repercussions in future elections.\nOn April 10, 2022, AMLO held the first recall referendum in the history of Mexico. Both Morena and the president\ncampaigned in favor of the referendum and AMLO remaining in charge but reduced the budget of the INE for\ncarrying out such a referendum from 18 million pesos to 4 million. The lack of a clear legal framework; the\nopacity of the referendum’s text, whether in favor of or against AMLO; the budget scandal; and the illegal active\nparticipation of the political party involved in the organization of the referendum affected its legitimacy and\ncitizens’ participation. Less than 20 percent of the population voted, which undermined the referendum’s legality,\nas it required a threshold of 40 percent to be constitutionally valid.137 Further, 91.86 percent of those who voted\nexpressed support for AMLO, who considered it a major victory. His reaction to the results also led many to\nspeculate about his hidden intent to use the referendum as an instrument to prolong his mandate after 2024.\nIn 2023 AMLO’s approval rate remained around 60 percent, according to most opinion polls.138 Evidently,\nmany are still attracted to AMLO’s welfare program and direct contact with citizens through his mañaneras\n(daily press conferences), seduced by his narrative of helping the poor against corruption and the elites. Many\nrevere the president’s personal austerity and common touch. Others have instead realized that the state of\nthe Mexican economy and security is far worse now than before AMLO came into office and that his welfare\nprogram conceals cuts to healthcare and education and presents financial irregularities, with evidence of\ncorruption and wasted resources.139\nIn the context of fast-approaching presidential elections, AMLO’s goal is one of maintaining popularity and\nensuring his party remains in power after he finishes his mandate. His challenge lies in institutionalizing the\nparty and forging a stable path for succession, while convincing the polarized electorate that Morena still\nincarnates the alternative to the political establishment. As of writing, Morena had not yet chosen its official\ncandidate for 2024, but two figures had emerged as the most likely to replace AMLO.140\nClaudia Sheinbaum, current mayor of Mexico City, is AMLO’s favorite and the most likely to continue his\nagenda and style. However, her support, even within Morena’s electorate, is hampered by scandals, accidents,\n25 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nand criticism of her management of the public transportation system in the Mexican capital.141 She could\nappeal to AMLO’s base but would likely struggle to reunite those who are disillusioned with Morena and are\nstarting to look for other options.\nMarcelo Ebrard, current minister of foreign affairs, is seen as the preferred candidate among Morena\nsupporters.142 He is perceived as a competent figure both within and outside Morena and is more independent\nfrom AMLO than Sheinbaum. He could easily appeal to the middle class and convince a broader electorate.143 If\nhe is not chosen as Morena’s candidate, he may run as an independent candidate, which would make the 2024\nelectoral campaign even more dynamic.\nAMLO has also indicated a few other candidates are fit for pursuing his political project: Adán Augusto López\nHernández, secretary of the interior, and Senator Ricardo Monreal Ávila.144 Given there is no established\nmechanism for party primaries, AMLO seems to prefer a set of opinion polls that will make it easier to vote\nfor Sheinbaum, even though such a procedure may trigger questions about transparency and lead to one of\nthe candidates running as an independent.145 Neither candidate, however, possesses the same charisma and\npersonal touch as AMLO, meaning Morena’s narrative could still shift away from AMLO’s.\nFigure 9: Potential Candidate Approval Ratings among Party Members\nMorena’s Coalition\nClaudia Sheinbaum\nMarcelo Ebrard\nRicardo Monreal\nGerardo Fernández Noroña\nAdán Augusto López\nVa Por México\nMiguel Ángel Mancera\nLilly Téllez\nSantiago Creel\nBeatriz Paredes\nClaudia Ruiz Massieu\nSilvano Aureoles\nMovimiento Ciudadano\nLuis Donaldo Colosio Riojas\nEnrique Alfaro\nSamuel García\n0% 5% 10% 15% 20% 25% 30% 35%\nSource: Jorge Buendía, “Encuesta: Morena sigue adelante y gana con cualquier corcholata” [Poll: Morena remains ahead and wins\nwith any aspirant], El Universal, May 5, 2022, https://www.eluniversal.com.mx/nacion/encuesta-morena-sigue-adelante-y-gana-con-\ncualquier-corcholata/.\n26 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nMorena has also benefited from the opposition not yet forming a cohesive alternative for Mexican citizens.\nThe opposition currently holds just two out of the ten most populous states and is experiencing difficulties in\ndefining its leader.146 Yet, PAN-PRI-PRD have confirmed that they will run together as the alliance called Va Por\nMéxico, and PAN will be in charge of finding the best candidates to challenge Morena.147 AMLO’s recent blatant\nattacks on Mexico’s democratic institutions have helped the opposition reorient and reframe its strategy. Polls\nfrom January 2023 reveal that the opposition currently has an approval rating of 39 percent.148 Further threats\nto democracy could make the opposition stronger and increase its popular support. If Movimiento Ciudadano\n(MC) were to join the opposition coalition, the chances of beating Morena would be much higher. MC could\ntip the balance given its policies are similar to those of PAN and PRI, it appeals to young urban voters, and it\ncurrently controls two of Mexico’s most important states.149\nTHE ELECTORAL OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nWhile the question of who will be president in 2024 cannot be answered with any degree of certainty, the June\n2023 gubernatorial elections for the State of Mexico and Coahuila nevertheless carry important implications. In\nthe State of Mexico, a shaky coalition among the opposition parties contributed to a win by Morena candidate\nDelfina Gómez, who garnered a 52.7 percent majority despite having been found guilty of stealing pay from\nfederal employees to support her party during a previous bid for governor. While Morena’s victory in a state\nlong viewed as a PRI bastion has been cast as a sign of the inevitability of a Morena president in 2024, the party’s\ndefeat by the opposition Va por México coalition in Coahuila suggests such predictions are far from set in stone\nand underscores the value of coalition-building and effective candidate selection as key ingredients going into\n2024.150 In Coahuila, Morena was beset by difficulties and demonstrated lack of discipline, unceremoniously\ndumping candidates and making last-minute adjustments to lists after lagging in the polls. The lack of unity\namong the opposition and current opinion polls still indicate the likelihood of a Morena 2.0 mandate, at least as\nfar as the presidency is concerned. Nevertheless, challenges persist for AMLO in picking a successor, with the\npossibility of an internal split within Morena. When it comes to Congress, Morena seems likely to lose ground,\nushering in a possibly contentious environment dominated by obstructionism, public dissent, and inability to\nadvance policy. Should a successor from Morena ascend to the presidency, they will also need to grapple with\nAMLO's legacy, which already promises to exercise significant influence as all the party's current aspirants voice\ntheir support for creating a new \"Ministry of the Fourth Transformation\" (potentially headed by AMLO's son) to\ncarry on the outgoing president's vision for the country.\nU.S.-Mexico Relations\nAMLO’s friendly attitude toward Trump from 2018 to 2020 obscured an anti-American agenda. This became\neven clearer once President Joseph Biden was elected and AMLO refused to congratulate him.151 The two\ncountries are now maintaining a “strained partnership” characterized by unsuccessful diplomatic efforts and\ncontroversies over energy policy.152 In fact, contrary to the equal treatment for private companies and path\ntoward renewable energy established in the USMCA, AMLO proceeded to nationalize energy companies and\nlithium production. Canadian and U.S. concerns ended up in formal dispute settlement mechanisms, which\nbegan in July 2022.\nAMLO faces a dilemma regarding the USMCA. Increased economic cooperation with the United States and\nCanada has been interpreted as a sign of stability, boosting international investments in Mexico.153 Yet AMLO’s\nanti-American sentiment has shaped his agenda and has been exploited as a political element. To him, personal\npopularity and the question of his successor matter more than the relationship with the United States.154\n27 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nMigration issues also heavily shape the bilateral relationship. During his administration, Trump threatened\nto impose 5 percent tariffs on imports from Mexico if the country did not get more involved in controlling\nmigration from Central America. An agreement was reached providing for Mexico’s contribution to controlling\nmigration in exchange for noninterference from the United States in internal Mexican issues and AMLO’s way\nof governing.155 With authorities counting 2.76 million undocumented migrants in 2022, Biden has underscored\nhow Trump’s compromise with AMLO does not work anymore, though he has been reluctant to address\nMexican internal affairs.156\nU.S. president Joe Biden (L) is welcomed by his Mexican counterpart AMLO upon landing at Felipe Ángeles\nInternational Airport in Zumpango de Ocampo, north of Mexico City on January 8, 2023.\nPhoto Source: Claudio Cruz/AFP/Getty Images\nAnother controversial area in the U.S.-Mexico relationship touches on drug enforcement cooperation and\nsecurity. Both countries appear to be talking at cross-purposes. The United States is deeply concerned with\ndrug trafficking, especially fentanyl coming from Mexico, which is responsible for the vast majority of the over\n107,000 overdose deaths reported in the United States for 2021.157 Mexico, for its part, is primarily concerned\nwith the flow of guns from the United States and recently sued the United States for alleged failure to curb\nillegal arms trafficking to Mexico.158 Cooperation in this field has been undermined by several actions taken by\nAMLO’s government. In 2020 the National Security Law responded to U.S. prosecution of Mexican officials for\ncollaboration with illegal drug traffickers by limiting the scope of action of U.S. drug enforcement agents on\nMexican territory. In 2021 the Mexican government shut down an investigative unit that worked closely with\nthe DEA, complicating the agency’s task of passing sensitive information to Mexican agents.159\nWith the new Bicentennial Framework launched in January 2022 replacing the Mérida Initiative, which defined\nU.S.-Mexico security cooperation for over a decade, Mexico and the United States have tried to address organized\ncrime less from a military perspective and more from a public health and developmental perspective, together with\nactions to target labs and extradite leaders.160 However, InSight Crime criticizes the plan for remaining very similar\nto the Mérida Initiative.161 The plan’s kingpin strategy, with its excessive focus on targeting leaders, might increase\ninsecurity, as followers are as important as leaders in avoiding fractures within and fights between drug cartels.162\n28 | Ryan C. Berg, Sara Fattori, and Henry Ziemer\n\nSeveral other episodes have yielded an uneven track record for the diplomatic relationship between Mexico\nand the United States. In 2022, for instance, AMLO boycotted the Summit of the Americas hosted in Los\nAngeles. Officially, he cited opposition to the barring of Nicaragua, Cuba, and Venezuela from the summit,\nthough his refusal was likely linked to a desire to avoid critics of Mexico’s internal affairs.163 The White House\nmeeting between AMLO and Biden in July 2022 reassured the existence and importance of Mexico-U.S. ties.\nDespite their different styles, Mexico and the United States agreed on the need to work more closely together,\nreduce barriers to trade, and tackle food insecurity and immigration. Immediately after the meeting, Mexico\nagreed to spend over $1.5 billion on border infrastructure between 2022 and 2024.164\nFollowing the U.S.-Mexico High-Level Economic Dialogue in 2022, the two countries committed to enhance\ntheir supply chains and reduce risk of disruptions by nearshoring the manufacturing of critical products, such\nas semiconductors and personal protective equipment (PPE), to Mexico and addressing critical minerals.165\nThe North American Leaders’ Summit in January 2023 was also deemed positive by observers for advancing\ncooperation, though it still took place under a cloud of tense trade negotiations.166\nDespite this gradual diplomatic rapprochement, the United States and Mexico appear ideologically divergent.\nAMLO has expressed a wish to see the Organization of American States reformed, accusing it of being\nexcessively dominated by the United States. The Mexico State Morena Youth group has supported the\nRussian invasion of Ukraine, while AMLO has criticized NATO’s actions. At the same time, Mexico has signed\nan agreement with Russia on space cooperation and has diplomatically supported dictatorships such as in\nVenezuela. This has further complicated every other aspect of the U.S.-Mexico partnership.167\nTHE BILATERAL OUTLOOK FOR 2024\nAlthough Biden has not been vocal that the U.S.-Mexico partnership is in danger and has opted not to criticize\nAMLO’s domestic practices, AMLO has brought Mexico ideologically further from the United States than it\nhas been in a long time. On the one hand, security cooperation and diplomacy have faced several ups and\ndowns that can be expected to continue in the final year of the AMLO and Biden administrations, given\ntheir inherently different styles and political agendas. On the other hand, trade and economic integration\nhave reached very high levels, with the two sides talking about aligning incentives in key industries such as\nsemiconductors. In 2022 Mexico was the United States’ second-largest trading partner in the trade of goods\nand services, while the United States accounted for 80 percent of Mexican goods exports.168 Recent increases\nin bilateral trade portend a trend that is likely to remain. In addition, with growing opportunities to relocate\nindustries from Asia to Latin America to avoid supply chain disruptions, Mexico has become key in attracting\ninvestors, underlining the expected expansion of U.S.-Mexico economic relations in the near future.169\nIn 2024 the core issues for the U.S.-Mexico relationship are anticipated to remain the same: Mexico and the\nUnited States will have to cooperate on trade, investment, full implementation of the USMCA, drug trafficking,\nsecurity, migration, and especially border control. However, the way in which such cooperation will manifest\ncould vary significantly. In 2024 both the United States and Mexico will elect new presidents. AMLO’s departure\ncould already be significant for the partnership, and no matter his success in designating a successor, whoever\nenters the Palacio Nacional next year will undoubtedly seek to leave their mark on Mexico’s foreign policy.\nMexican domestic and foreign policy—in particular the extent to which Mexico manages to control its borders—\ncould, in turn, affect U.S. elections, as border policy remains one of the Republican Party’s main concerns.\nMeanwhile, proponents within the U.S. Congress who have pushed legislative initiatives authorizing military\nforce, including air strikes, against criminal organizations operating in Mexico would do well to remember one\nanalyst’s observation: “Mexico is a target-rich environment, not for U.S. missiles, but for sanctions.”170\n29 | After AMLO: The Economic, Security, and Political Outlook for Mexico in 2024\n\nIn addition, political relations will determine the extent to which Mexico can secure nearshoring\nopportunities, as well as investments in minerals, semiconductors, and other key industries that now seem to\nbroaden the potential of U.S.-Mexico economic relations. At the same time, Mexico may continue to pursue\nits anti-American sentiment aiming to avoid undue interference in internal affairs and seeking the support\nof countries with similar agendas.171 The more the country becomes militarized and the less secure and\ndemocratic it becomes, the more its relations with the United States could be eroded.172 In any case, Mexico\nwill still need pragmatism, as it cannot neglect its dependence on access to the U.S. market and the great\neconomic and trade opportunities established under the USMCA. Ultimately, geographic proximity, economic\ninterdependence, and positive day-to-day cooperation will play a role in keeping the two countries close,\noutlining a bright spot in an otherwise gloomy picture.\n30 | Ryan C. Por lo tanto, en este capítulo se hace un análisis de las poblaciones objetivo aten-\n     didas por los 16 programas prioritarios vigentes en 2023, a partir de los factores relevantes contenidos\n     en sus objetivos de Propósito.\n\n\n     ¿Qué grupos poblacionales están atendiendo los programas prioritarios?\n\n     La clave para lograr un desarrollo social efectivo comienza con la identificación precisa del problema\n     público que los programas se proponen resolver. El siguiente paso crucial es determinar qué pobla-\n     ción objetivo se va a atender con base en los criterios de elegibilidad establecidos en la normativa del\n     programa y en qué medida se espera lograr el cambio o resultado. En relación con los 16 programas\n     prioritarios vigentes para 2023, la gráfica 3 revela que 81 % (13) identifica adecuadamente a su pobla-\n     ción objetivo, mientras que 19 % (3) aún no.\n\n\n     Gráfica 3. ¿Cuántos programas prioritarios identifican su población objetivo en 2023?\n\n\n\n\n                                             19% (3)\n\n\n                                                                                                     El programa identifica adecuadamente\n                                                                                                     a su población objetivo\n\n                                                                                                     El programa no identifica a su población objetivo\n\n                                                                              81% (13)\n\n\n\n\n     Fuente: elaboración del CONEVAL.\n\n\n\n     A partir de la población objetivo que aparece en el Propósito de cada programa social, en 2023 se\n     identificaron siete grupos poblacionales, mismos que se muestran en la figura 4. De los tres programas que coordina\n     Sader (S290 “Precios de Garantía a Productos Alimentarios Básicos”, S292 “Fertilizantes” y S293 “Produc-\n     ción para el Bienestar”), sus Propósitos señalan a pequeños y medianos productores rurales.\n\n\n     Por su parte, el programa S287 “Sembrando Vida” (Bienestar) también pertenece a la categoría Produc-\n     tores, no obstante que, como se muestra en la figura 5, en su Propósito identifica como población obje-\n     tivo a “las/los sujetos de derecho”, término ambiguo y no armonizado con lo establecido en sus Reglas\n     de Operación (ROP),9 donde sí se delimita una población objetivo con características específicas.\n\n\n\n     9\n          Las ROP del programa están disponibles en la siguiente liga: https://dof.gob.mx/nota_detalle.php?codigo=5676230&fecha=30/12/2022#gsc.tab=0\n\n\n\n\n18       Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2023\n         Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social\n\nFigura 5. Propósito del programa S287 “Sembrando Vida”\n\n\n\n                                                                          S287 - Sembrando Vida (Bienestar)\n\n             Los/las sujetos de derecho con ingresos inferiores a la línea de pobreza en localidades rurales cuentan con 2.5 hectá-\n                                reas produciendo en SIAF y MIAF, con necesidades alimenticias básicas cubiertas.\n\n     Fuente: elaboración del CONEVAL.\n\n\n\n     Después de los Productores, la categoría que condensa más programas es la de No identifican con\n     claridad a su población objetivo. De los 16 programas prioritarios, a 3 (18 %) se les dificulta detectar\n     con claridad la población que buscan atender. Cabe precisar que, para definir y delimitar la po-\n     blación objetivo, el Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social (CONEVAL)\n     siempre recalca la importancia de que los programas se basen en la Metodología de Marco Lógico\n     (MML) para que estos midan resultados en la población que atienden y, de esta manera, se fortalezca\n     la rendición de cuentas.\n\n\n     A continuación, la figura 6 describe los objetivos de Propósito de los tres programas que presentan\n     áreas de oportunidad en la identificación de su población objetivo.\n\n\n     Figura 6. Objetivo de Propósito de los programas U083, S281, U013, 2023\n\n\n\n                                                   U083 - Universidades para el Bienestar Benito Juárez García (SEP)\n\n\n              Superar la exclusión educativa de tipo superior contribuye a disminuir la desigualdad en comunidades y municipios.\n\n\n\n                                                           S281 - Programa Nacional de Reconstrucción (Sedatu)\n\n\n             Los municipios afectados por los sismos de septiembre de 2017 y febrero de 2018 recuperan los inmuebles de salud,\n                               educación, vivienda y bienes culturales dañados, para beneficio de la población.\n\n\n                    U013 - Atención a la Salud y Medicamentos Gratuitos para la Población sin Seguridad Social Laboral (Salud)\n\n\n\n                                                  Se cuenta con transferencia de recursos a las Entidades Federativas.\n\n\n     Fuente: elaboración del CONEVAL.\n\n\n\n     Como se aprecia, los programas U013 y U083 no especifican ningún grupo poblacional al cual bus-\n     can atender. El U013 tampoco precisa cuál es el resultado o cambio que se propone, dado que su\n     objetivo se limita a la entrega de un bien (transferencia), lo cual no es un resultado sino un tema de\n     Gestión. En lugar de identificar un problema público a solucionar y la población afectada por este\n     problema, solo se distribuye un bien, lo que es distintivo de un Componente de un programa y no del\n\n\n\n19   Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2023\n     Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social\n\nobjetivo central, del motor y eje de un programa. Por su parte, el objetivo que plantea el programa\n     U083 es más apropiado para un objetivo de Fin, pues menciona el resultado al que busca contribuir\n     (“disminuir la desigualdad en comunidades y municipios”).\n\n\n     Es completa responsabilidad del programa resolver la problemática central que se plantea como Pro-\n     pósito. La exclusión educativa de tipo superior es un objetivo muy amplio, por lo resulta confuso qué\n     es lo que el programa persigue. Los derechos sociales a la educación y a la salud son fundamentales\n     para el bienestar de la población mexicana; por lo tanto, como dichos programas son indispensables\n     para hacer efectiva su garantía, es importante que el diseño del Propósito de los tres programas se\n     apegue a la MML.\n\n\n     Por su parte, el programa S281 señala a los “municipios afectados por los sismos”, sin especificar el\n     tipo de afectación ni las características que deben cumplir los municipios para acceder a los apoyos.\n     Cabe recordar que los programas sociales se crean para resolver problemas que afectan a personas,\n     los cuales, en el caso de los programas prioritarios, son de carácter urgente pues son graves o afectan\n     a poblaciones vulnerables. Si bien el daño a la infraestructura de salud, educación, vivienda y bienes\n     culturales de los municipios afectados por los sismos es, en efecto, un problema grave, quien sufre es-\n     tos daños es la población que habita en esos municipios, la cual presenta características particulares,\n     ya sea jóvenes o adultos mayores, personas indígenas o afromexicanas, que habiten en localidades\n     con alto rezago social o cuyos ingresos sean insuficientes para acceder a servicios privados de las\n     vertientes que el programa atiende. Por ello, es clave que este programa identifique adecuadamente\n     cuál es su población objetivo.\n\n\n     Como ejemplo de lo anterior, la figura 7 muestra el Propósito del S273 “Programa de Mejoramiento\n     Urbano (PMU)”, coordinado igualmente por Sedatu. En su objetivo central, tanto la población que\n     atiende como el resultado que espera están claramente identificados. Al delimitar su población ob-\n     jetivo considerando elementos como el rezago urbano y social, se comprende cuál es el problema\n     que la aqueja y su dimensión. Así, el programa cuenta con elementos para reportar sus resultados de\n     manera oportuna.\n\n\n     Figura 7. Objetivo de Propósito del programa S273 “Programa de Mejoramiento Urbano (PMU)”\n\n\n\n                                                     S273 - Programa de Mejoramiento Urbano (PMU) (Sedatu)\n\n\n            La población ubicada en manzanas con medio, alto y muy alto grado de rezago urbano y social en las ciudades de\n             15,000 habitantes o más que forman parte del Sistema Urbano Nacional (SUN) 2018, reducen sus condiciones\n                                                     de rezago urbano y social.\n\n     Fuente: elaboración del CONEVAL.\n\n\n\n\n20   Informe de monitoreo de programas prioritarios 2023\n     Consejo Nacional de Evaluación de la Política de Desarrollo Social", "excerpt_sha256": "5f37cc5c12c795a74582acaf27036c0c7149a8385596b6b38310b0f362d5d1e1", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports all stated program-design findings and the 13-of-16/three-program count.", "The excerpt does not establish personal responsibility by López Obrador, but the summary attributes the finding to CONEVAL."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0052-E010", "source_id": "SRC-8cd79037d1d87b97", "url": "https://mexico.un.org/sites/default/files/2023-09/Results%20Report%202022%20COMPRESSED.pdf", "title": "UN Mexico/UNICEF 2022 results PDF", "publisher": "United Nations Mexico", "raw_sha256": "f25d1cdb960f4f6370e998c44f566b644d19c73cfa5062f3911d41bf48208493", "fact_summary": "UN-supported cooperation helped enroll more than 54,000 domestic workers in IMSS, supported foundations for a National Care System, strengthened laws and services addressing violence against women, and supported school reopening, teacher training, learning recovery, and dropout reintegration. The government requested restructuring or cancellation of multiple projects, while policy lending and analytical support continued.", "question_ids": ["5B.10", "5B.6", "5B.7", "6B.5", "6B.9", "8B.2", "8B.3", "8B.4", "8B.5", "8B.6", "8B.7", "8B.8", "8B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The review was prepared in 2023 and evaluates the FY2020-2025 partnership at its midpoint.", "ruler_attribution": "The review explicitly identifies client-requested cancellations, changing government priorities, and institutional capacity as factors, providing direct but not exclusive attribution to the administration.", "limitations": ["CPF indicators measure World Bank partnership performance, not the whole Mexican state.", "The review combines outcomes from different years and projects and includes external shocks."], "start_unit": 9, "end_unit": 15, "locator": "page 9 through page 15", "exact_excerpt": "III. SUMMARY OF PROGRAM IMPLEMENTATION\nProgram and Portfolio Performance\n13. World Bank lending to Mexico has increased, driven by high growth in policy lending. IBRD lending\nhas increased by more than 25 percent in this CPF period relative to the previous one (FY14-19), propelled\nby an eightfold increase in DPF net commitments. At the end of FY23, total approved lending during the CPF\nperiod was US$4.3 billion (US$3.9 billion in DPF), versus US$3.4 billion during the previous CPF (US$0.5 billion\nin DPF). One additional DPF is planned for FY24. IFC’s commitments increased from US$1.62 billion in FY14-\n17 to US$1.75 billion in FY20-23. Annex 5 provides an overview of portfolio performance.\n14. Higher demand for policy lending and reduced appetite for investment lending can be traced to a\ncombination of global challenges and domestic policy shifts. Multiple global crises made fast-disbursing DPF\noperations the instrument of choice. Domestically, policy priorities were focused on a few large public\ninvestment projects, limiting space in the federal budget for other CPF focus areas. The government also\nwithdrew earlier requests for World Bank support in several areas, indicating shifting priorities or views of\nthe benefits of external project financing. Changing policy directions, institutional challenges, high turnover\nin key institutions, and security obstacles further affected policy continuity, implementation, and results.\nWith the 2024 electoral transition approaching, the window of opportunity for new policy reforms and the\npreparation of any further IPF pipeline is now limited.29\n15. The World Bank showed flexibility and pragmatism to deliver results through two main\nengagement strategies. First, IBRD responded to changing policy priorities by focusing on policy lending\nduring COVID-19 while continuing to invest in selected shared priorities: forestry preservation, financial\ninclusion, an improved business environment, and gender equality. IFC introduced upstream pre-investment\nprograms to support market creation and project structuring from an early stage, complemented by advisory\nprojects on climate, gender, and digitalization. Second, at client request, several investment operations were\nrestructured by the Bank, with cancellations of US$894 million out of US$1.28 billion in net commitments.\n16. The World Bank has maintained a strong analytical program to inform DPFs, to add value where\nmore intensive financial engagement was not possible, and to explore new entry-points. Demand-driven\nAdvisory Services and Analytics (ASA) informed key issues across the CPF objectives and maintained World\nBank engagement in many areas in the absence of lending (e.g., education, labor market policy, climate\nchange). The Reimbursable Advisory Services (RAS) instrument – a powerful instrument for middle-income\ncountries like Mexico – has been deployed in the transport sector, with a new agreement signed in July 2023.\n17. The CPF’s intended subnational focus on Mexico’s southern states was advanced through policy\nlending and advisory services. The CPF aimed for a greater impact on Mexico’s southern states, where a\nlarge share of the poorest population lives. This objective was affected by the reduced IPF program to\nchannel further resources to the poorest states in the south, as legal and institutional obstacles prevent direct\nlending to subnational government in Mexico. Still, IBRD supported federal policies (through policy-based\nlending) that generated benefits for the whole country, but had positive effects for lagging states (e.g., the\nUrban Upgrading Program supported under the Environmental Sustainability and Urban Resilience DPF\ntargeted marginalized urban areas with above-average deficits in access to public services and infrastructure\nin southern states). Analytical and advisory engagements have allowed demand-driven support to states, for\nexample through just-in-time support in several sectors (e.g., productivity, education, disaster risk\nmanagement), helping identify new partners and pave the way for potential future engagements (Annex 5).\n18. Fiduciary management has been satisfactory. Financial management and procurement\narrangements for IBRD projects are adequate. IBRD has strengthened client procurement capacity and\n29 The choice of lending instrument also relates to the characteristics of the investment project financing (IPF) instrument, particularly\nits disbursement profile, associated commitment fees and implementation modalities.\n4\n\nharmonized procurement documents are being updated.30 IBRD has also supported higher quality financial\nmanagement at national and project levels, in coordination with the Inter-American Development Bank (IDB)\nand the national organized accounting profession. Audits are generally on time and unqualified. More\ncustomized, agile approaches, including greater use of digital technologies, will be explored.\n19. The portfolio complied with safeguard policies and the Environmental and Social Framework (ESF).\nProject environmental and social risk ratings have ranged from moderate to low. Targeted supervision and\ncapacity-strengthening for all IPF projects have advanced ESF implementation, including in good practices\nsuch as the Dedicated Grant Mechanism for Indigenous Peoples and Local Communities Project.\n20. IFC’s committed portfolio in Mexico stands at US$2.4 billion, leveraging further private-sector\nfinance. As of December 31st, 2023, IFC portfolio included US$827 million of mobilized resources, the fourth\nlargest portfolio regionally and 13th globally. From FY20 to February 2024, IFC has committed US$2.3 billion\nof long-term financing (including US$900 million of mobilized resources) and US$706 million of short-term\nfinancing.\n21. MIGA’s guarantees in Mexico stand at US$2.3 billion, de-risking foreign investments and lending,\nwhich could lead the way to structure more operations with the financial sector. Mexico is MIGA’s largest\nexposure in the LAC region and globally. As of end-FY23, MIGA’s exposure in Mexico was in support of one\nproject in the energy sector. During the CPF, MIGA has provided guarantees to the Federal Electricity\nCommission to rehabilitate and modernize hydropower plants and to the export credit agency Bancomext to\nhelp exporting firms mitigate the impacts of the pandemic, the latter cancelled in April 2023. In October 2023,\nMIGA issued capital optimization guarantees of $1.9 billion to HSBC, covering the risk of expropriation of\nfunds for the mandatory reserves that HSBC Mexico holds at the central bank of Mexico.\nProgress Towards CPF Objectives\n22. Despite progress, many CPF targets are not on track. The global and domestic shifts described above\nhave both affected CPF implementation and results. Of 24 objective indicators, (i) eight have been achieved,\n(ii) one is partially achieved, (iii) five are on track but require adjustments to be achieved, and (iv) ten are off\ntrack, five of which are unlikely to be achieved as a result of cancellations or restructurings.31 In some areas,\nprogress has been made through other channels, e.g., DPF or ASA as opposed to IPF (see Annexes 3 and 5).\nFocus Area A: Supporting more rapid, more inclusive growth.\n23. In Focus Area A, of six indicators, one is achieved, one is partially achieved, one is on track, and\nthree are off track. Challenges are mainly due to cancellations and restructurings of operations, pandemic\nimpact, and monetary tightening to contain inflation.\n24. Financial intermediation and inclusion results are mixed. Progress on expanding access to credit for\nMSMEs was constrained by the pandemic, as well as liquidity and solvency issues faced by non-banking\nfinancial institutions (Indicator 1.2: MSMEs loans extended). Additional IBRD financing of the “Expanding\nRural Finance Project” was ruled out by debt ceilings during the pandemic and a shift in policy and lending\npriorities (Indicator 1.1: rural microenterprises and SMEs with access to credit). Financial inclusion has been\nexpanded through electronic payments via the new electronic payment platform (Indicator 1.3).\n25. IBRD has supported subnational competition regulation in the telecom, transport, and retail\nsectors. Forty-one subnational laws and regulations were changed to introduce a more competitive\nregulatory framework, achieving Indicator 2.1 (pro-competitive regulatory framework at the subnational\n30 Seven IPFs, including four trust funds are implementing the procurement regulations, taking into account the benefits of their\nflexibilities according to the context, risk, value, and complexity of each project, supported by its own project procurement strategy.\n31 Achieved: the targets were met on time or ahead of time; Partially achieved: half of the targets or less were met and the indicator\nis dropped in the PLR; On track: more than half of the targets have been met; Off track: half of the targets or less have been met.\n5\n\nlevel). IBRD also supported the conceptualization and design of the national digital platform aimed at\nenhancing business regulation across the regulatory governance cycle. IFC support to Altan Redes (the\ncompany responsible for the shared network) contributed to an increase in the population covered by\nwireless broadband to 70 percent as of January 2022 (Indicator 2.2). Limited progress was observed on\nagriculture storage infrastructure and market price information systems (Indicator 2.3), as a result of the\nJanuary 2020 cancellation of the “Grain Storage and Information for Agricultural Competitiveness Project” at\nclient request, reflecting changes in priorities. A strong ASA program on inclusive growth included the 2022\nProductivity Report and joint IBRD-IFC work on nearshoring, entrepreneurship, and eco-industrial parks.\nFocus Area B: Strengthening institutions for public finance, service delivery, and economic inclusion.\n26. In Focus Area B, of nine indicators, three have been achieved, one is on track, and five are off track.\nOn public service delivery, under Objective 3 (enhance the management of public resources), no progress\nwas made on Indicator 3.1 (population captured by the single identification system) due to the client decision\nto cancel the “National Digital Identity System Project” in 2022, reflecting priority changes. Support to public\nfinance through the “Modernization of Public Financial Management Systems in Mexico Project” was\nconstrained by the pandemic and implementation delays, which limited progress on Indicators 3.2 (budget\nexecuted through the Integrated Financial Management Information System) and 3.3 (time to produce\nconsolidated budget execution information). The project was restructured and is now in problem project\nstatus. IFC’s support to Public Private Partnership (PPP) transactions included two projects successfully\nawarded in May 2021 under the Los Cabos Water Advisory Project (Indicator 3.4: PPP transactions).\n27. Despite the impact of the pandemic, specific results indicators in education and social protection\nservice delivery are encouraging. Objective 4 (strengthen institutional capacity to deliver inclusive social\nservices) has been supported through the national early childhood development strategy (indicator 4.1\nadoption at the state level). Indicator 4.2 (pilots of the new integrated health networks at the state level) was\nnot achieved due to changes in government priorities. IBRD technical assistance was provided to the National\nInstitute of Public Health and the Secretariat of Finance and Public Credit (SHCP). Almost 52,000 additional\npatients were attended between 2018 and 2022 by IFC supported healthcare services, exceeding the targets\nfor Indicator 4.3 (patients benefiting from the healthcare services). Objective 5 (strengthen the capacity of\nthe social protection system for economic inclusion) was achieved through the “Social Protection System\nProject” and support to Mexico’s National Employment Service (SNE). Indicator 5.1 (Integrated Social\nProtection Centers established) was surpassed. Indicator 5.2 (implementation of modernized processes at\nSNE) has also been achieved through support to SNE, including the expansion of its office network.\nFocus Area C: Enabling sustainable infrastructure and climate action.\n28. In Focus Area C four of the nine objective indicators have been achieved; three are on track (after\nsome adjustments); and two are off track. Notable results have been achieved on housing and sustainable\nlandscape management, despite some implementation delays and restructuring.\n29. Progress has been lower than expected under Objective 6 on more inclusive and sustainable\ninfrastructure services. The “Water Security and Resilience of the Valley of Mexico Project” (PROSEGHIR),\napproved in February 2020, was delayed for over two years, slowing progress on Indicator 6.2 (people with\nimproved water supply reliability), but remains a priority in line with the Evolution Roadmap’s global\nchallenge on water security and access. A project to support Mexico’s National Infrastructure Fund\n(FONADIN) was dropped during preparation, affecting Indicator 6.1 (private capital mobilization for\ninfrastructure). Nevertheless, almost US$570 million have been mobilized by the issuance of sustainable\nbonds with a gender perspective by the infrastructure development bank, BANOBRAS. Despite US$800\nmillion of IFC additional private finance mobilization and own account investments in infrastructure, results\nare below expectations for indicator 6.1. MIGA’s guarantee played a catalytic role in the Tierra Mojada Power\nPlant project, which was cancelled in December 2020 owing to the successful refinancing of the project’s\n6\n\nbank debt with a bond, issued after the project had become operational. Although minimum power was\ndelivered during MIGA’s contract period, the project has generated 5,828 GWh of electricity above the target\nfor Indicator 6.4 (total power generated). In June 2023, MIGA also signed a guarantee supporting CFE for the\nrehabilitation and modernization of hydropower plants. The “Energy Efficiency in Public Facilities Project”\n(PRESEMEH) was scaled down to one third of the original US$150 million, reducing progress on Indicators 6.3\n(energy savings) and 7.1 (GHG emissions reduced). Indicator 6.5 (access to low-cost resilient housing) was\nreached through the “Improving Access to Affordable Housing Project” and IFC support to a housing provider.\n30. Progress on Objective 7 on climate change mitigation has been satisfactory. Sustainable landscape\nmanagement practices (Indicator 7.2) and the number of beneficiaries, particularly women and indigenous\npeoples, implementing sustainable management schemes (Indicator 7.3) have increased with satisfactory\nimplementation of the “Strengthening Entrepreneurship in Productive Forest Landscapes Project”\n(PROFOEM). Similarly, power generated from renewable energy sources supported by IFC has achieved its\ntarget (Indicator 7.4), with potential for further support. Indicators 7.1 (GHG emissions) and 7.3 (sustainable\nmanagement schemes in rural areas) were affected by restructurings of the forest, sustainable landscapes,\nand energy projects, but remain a priority to help tackle the global challenge of climate change.\nProgress Towards Corporate Commitments\n31. Corporate commitments have been mainstreamed across World Bank operations. The portfolio has\nexceeded corporate and regional targets since FY21 with 100 percent of IBRD projects gender-tagged and all\nactive projects complying with citizen engagement requirements. On climate, IBRD reached an average of\n41.3 percent of co-benefits in FY21-23, and IFC and MIGA had an average of 54.5 and 44.0 percent\nrespectively of new projects and guarantees tagged as climate-related since FY20 (Annex 6).\nPartnerships and Leveraging\n32. The World Bank has deepened coordination with other development partners. Under the\n“Environment Sustainability and Urban Resilience DPF” prior actions on climate, forests, and environmental\nmanagement were coordinated with partners (Annex 5). The Partnership for Market Implementation, a\nmulti-donor initiative led by the World Bank,32 has provided technical assistance on carbon pricing. The\nUnited Kingdom, the United States, and IBRD have joined forces to support Mexico’s forestry program\n(Objective 7). For the remainder of the CPF, closer collaboration within the WBG and with the IDB is expected,\nincluding on selected global challenges and private capital mobilization.\nIV. EMERGING LESSONS\n33. CPF design needs to adjust to the policy framework and operating environment, taking a\npragmatic, flexible, and creative approach to opportunities and risks. A realistic assessment of country\ncontext – policy priorities, financing constraints, political economy, and implementation capacity and\nlikelihood – is crucial. While the pandemic was impossible to predict, other governance, institutional, and\nsectoral risks could have been assessed more realistically and the program designed accordingly. The PLR\nbuilds on these lessons, with greater program selectivity and a more realistic risk assessment, seeking\nopportunities and balancing what is desirable with what is politically and technically possible at the federal,\nsubnational and sector levels. The Evolution Roadmap, with an enhanced value proposition for middle-\nincome countries, opens opportunities for a more integrated one World Bank Group approach at the project\nlevel and in joint knowledge engagements, such as on nearshoring, climate finance, financial inclusion, digital,\nenergy supply reliability and water. Global knowledge initiatives (e.g., the World Development Report on\nmigration) under the new Knowledge Compact may open doors to new forms of collaboration.\n34. Policy-lending can provide rapid support in response to crises, helping finance public expenditures\n32 Among them the European Commission, Germany, Spain, Sweden, Japan, United Kingdom, United States, and Australia.\n7\n\nand laying the foundations for a resilient recovery, while fostering innovations. In recent decades, Mexico\nhas followed a pattern of countercyclical lending, increasing exposure in downturns and crises (e.g., new\ncommitments of US$10.5 billion in FY08-10 and US$3.6 billion FY20-22). During COVID-19, three of four DPFs\napproved in FY20-22 directly supported pandemic crisis response and recovery, including the first “Build Back\nBetter” DPF. DPFs also supported innovative policy reforms (e.g., fintech, gender, and sustainable finance).\n35. IPF preparation can be challenging and fragile, yet investment lending operations have usually led\nto positive outcomes when implemented. Despite an investment pipeline reflecting the strength of the\ndialogue in many sectors, the government made a series of decisions not to proceed with many IPF\noperations. Complex project design, including inter- and intra-institutional coordination challenges,\nimplementation delays, partial cancellations, have jeopardized impact. Of the five IPF projects that closed in\nFY20-23, three were found satisfactory by the IEG (e.g., simple project design and client ownership\ncontributed to successful implementation). Four additional IPF projects are rated moderately satisfactory or\nbetter and are expected to achieve their objectives by closing in FY25. Reduced appetite for IPF and\nrestrictions on the use of this and other instruments (e.g., PforR) call for a close dialogue on design features,\nimplementation challenges, lessons learned to develop a new federal and potential also subnational pipeline.\n36. Analytical work has added technical value to policy making and served as a technical foundation\nfor policy lending. In the absence of new investment lending, the ASA program has positioned the World\nBank as a trusted adviser on evidence-based policy reforms, strengthening DPF design. Expansion of financial\naccess in Mexico was supported through the Financial Inclusion DPF, the COVID-19 Financial Access DPF, and\nthe Promoting Women’s Economic Opportunities and Sustainable Productivity Growth DPF. These operations\nbuilt on the 2016 and 2022 Financial Sector Assessment Programs, the Financial Inclusion Global Initiative,\nthe Gender Report, and IFC support on business regulations and SME access to credit.\n37. For IFC, deepening the relationship with the Ministry of Finance, diversifying business\ndevelopment efforts across industries and client types, while also emphasizing non-financial benefits, is\nexpected to both mitigate sector concentration risk and strengthen IFC’s value proposition. IFC investments\nin renewable energy in Mexico have grown substantially, representing almost 100 percent of new IFC\ninfrastructure investments from FY18 to FY20. Subsequent difficulties in the sector created a shock to IFC’s\ninfrastructure target, which was only partially mitigated by increased support for digital infrastructure. IFC’s\ndiversification to new players within the financial system and its use of the highest environmental, social, and\ngovernance standards have enhanced IFC’s contribution to the financial inclusion and climate agenda,\nincluded under CPF focus areas A and C. The latter can be illustrated by IFC investments in the Sustainable\nBond issued by GF Mega, structured under a sustainability framework to promote financial inclusion,\nwomen’s empowerment, and climate-friendly practices; and the Partial Credit Guarantee to IDEI Green Bond\nIssuance, to finance Excellence in Design for Greater Efficiencies (EDGE)-certified green residential projects.\nSynergies and complementarities among IFC, IBRD, and MIGA can also strengthen product and market\ndiversification.\nV. ADJUSTMENTS TO COUNTRY PARTNERSHIP FRAMEWORK\n38. This PLR confirms the strategic direction and the duration of the CPF but proposes adjustments to\nthe results framework. The PLR confirms that the three CPF Focus Areas remain appropriate to address\nMexico’s structural growth, inclusion, and sustainability challenges identified in the Systematic Country\nDiagnostic.33 The PLR adjusts Objective 2 to reflect new engagements with the private sector and drops\nObjective 3 to reflect implementation delays and cancellations. Objective 2 includes new indicators on the\nadoption of a framework to certify industrial parks. Focus Area 3 will continue supporting Mexico in some of\nthe most pressing global challenges, e.g., Climate Change Adaptation and Mitigation; Energy Access; and\nWater Security and Access. The results matrix has been streamlined to reflect reform space, client demand,\n33 World Bank, “Mexico Systematic Country Diagnostic,” August 2019.\n8\n\nand implementation capacity by sector34 (Annex 2).\nFocus Area A: Supporting more rapid, more inclusive growth.\n39. The PLR maintains Objective 1 to “foster financial intermediation and inclusion” and continues\nsupporting sustainable finance. With limited space for new lending, the PLR proposes to support Mexico’s\nsustainable finance agenda through analytical support, including the development of a biodiversity taxonomy\nand associated regulations, building on the joint IFC-IBRD 30x30 Zero advisory services, which also aims to\nraise the share of green assets in bank portfolios. On financial intermediation, new objectives have been\nadded on increasing account ownership and reducing the gender gap in digital payments. Indicators on users\nand payments through the digital payments’ platform and MSME loans have been revised to reflect progress.\n40. Objective 2 is adjusted from aiming to “reduce regulatory and competition barriers to economic\ngrowth” to “support private sector investment.” IBRD will support ASA on the subnational implementation\nof a national digital platform to improve business regulation. Based on analytical work on productivity,\nentrepreneurship, and eco-industrial parks, IBRD and IFC will support FDI attraction for the piloting of eco-\nindustrial parks. The PLR includes two new indicators on a framework to certify industrial parks including\nenvironmental sustainability and the number of IFC transactions tagged as “nearshoring.”\nFocus Area B: Strengthening institutions for service delivery and inclusion.\n41. Focus Area B is adjusted from “Strengthening institutions for public finance, service delivery, and\neconomic inclusion” to “Strengthening institutions for service delivery and inclusion.” The PLR drops\nObjective 3 to “enhance the management of public resources” due to project implementation delays and\ncancellations. IBRD will continue supporting SHCP plans to strengthen human resource and public investment\nmanagement and its integration with the budget system as well as the implementation of Mexico’s\nsustainable finance strategy. IFC will continue structuring and financing PPP projects in key sectors, yet with\nmore limited scope, given reduced federal funding to structure projects and uneven subnational capacities.\n42. Objective 4 (new Objective 3) is revised to “strengthen the capacity to deliver inclusive education\nand health services.” IBRD will remain engaged in early childhood education and support the recovery of\nlearning losses by (i) improving teaching practices; (ii) developing early warning systems to identify students\nat risk of dropping out and targeted efforts to lower this risk; and (iii) catalyzing human capital formation by\nexpanding early childhood care and education services. IFC will continue to support innovative, scalable\nmodels for high-quality, affordable private health-care services. In line with the pandemic prevention and\npreparedness global challenge, IBRD has shifted its engagement to formulating proposals to enhance\nreadiness and responses for health crises and the resilience of health financing to population ageing.\n43. Objective 5 (new Objective 4) is adjusted to “strengthen the capacity to deliver inclusive social\nservices.” IBRD work with SNE to use good practices and digital tools to support social protection and\nimprove the integration of vulnerable members of the labor force (e.g., women, youth, and migrants).\nFocus Area C: Enabling sustainable infrastructure and climate action.\n44. Objective 6 (new Objective 5) on more inclusive and sustainable infrastructure services remains a\npriority, despite challenges in infrastructure sectors. In the water sector, IBRD will continue to support water\nsupply and sanitation activities through its operation in the Valley of Mexico and potential investments and\nadvisory work in the northern part of the country (e.g., wastewater treatment and reuse, groundwater\nmonitoring and management, and irrigation modernization) in line with the water security and access global\nchallenge. The energy efficiency project (PRESEMEH) will be closed. MIGA will expand its presence through\nits guarantee to CFE supporting efforts towards tackling the global challenge on energy efficiency and access.\nIBRD will support Mexico’s development banks on private sector capital mobilization, especially for\n34 The PLR drops nine, adjusts thirteen, and reconfirms two out of 24 objective indicators, and adds five new indicators.\n9\n\ninfrastructure and energy. IFC will support competitive private energy markets and digital infrastructure,\nfocusing on renewables and telecom, in addition to other areas where private investment can help meeting\nthe growing needs from nearshoring. WBG collaboration with financial entities and funds will be\nstrengthened to promote private capital mobilization to address infrastructure needs in key sectors such as\ntransport, energy, water, and real estate. Indicators have been adjusted to reflect progress and challenges.\n45. The World Bank will intensify efforts to support Mexico’s climate change goals under Objective 7\n(new Objective 6). Cross-sectoral climate change mitigation and adaptation, one of the key global challenges\noutlined in the Evolution Roadmap, will be supported through ASA. IFC will mobilize knowledge and\ninvestments targeted at financial institutions, fintech, and funds to develop sound climate portfolios. MIGA\nwill seek opportunities to support renewable energy projects. MIGA has also issued new capital optimization\nguarantees to HSBC covering the risk of expropriation of funds for the mandatory reserves that HSBC Mexico\nholds at the central bank of Mexico. The benefits of the MIGA-enabled capital relief will provide financing of\nUS$1.5 billion to climate finance sub-projects over 2023-2026. IBRD operations on forestry and agriculture\nwill continue with a focus on forest-dependent communities and on strengthening community and producer\norganizations in the management and entrepreneurship of productive landscapes, protecting biodiversity\nand nature as a key global challenge. The World Bank will seek innovation in financial solutions for disaster\nmanagement and climate resilience and support the sustainable taxonomy. Objectives have been adjusted.\nVI. RISKS TO THE CPF PROGRAM\n46. The overall risk for this CPF has increased from Moderate to Substantial. Table shows the risk\nassessment, using the Systematic Operations Risk-rating Tool (SORT). The World Bank will carefully assess\nand mitigate risks through the electoral transition to build a close engagement with the new administration.\nTable 1. SORT: Risks to the CPF program\nRisk categories CPF Rating PLR Rating\n47. Political and Governance Risk is\nPolitical and governance M S\nincreased from moderate to substantial.\nMacroeconomic M M\nSectoral strategies and policies S S Program implementation could be delayed\nTechnical design of projects or programs M M ahead of the presidential election in June\nInstitutional capacity for implementation 2024 and the following transition period,\nM S\nand sustainability\nincluding restrictions on new reforms. To\nFiduciary M M\nmitigate this risk, IBRD will focus on: (i)\nEnvironment and social M M\naccelerating portfolio implementation and\nStakeholders M M\nOther (crime and violence) S S addressing project risks; (ii) preparing a\nOverall M S transition engagement strategy (including\nNote: Summary risks: H = high; S = substantial; M = moderate; L = low transition notes) and rapidly engaging with\nthe new administration; and (iii) seeking opportunities for subnational dialogue.\n48. Institutional Capacity for Implementation and Sustainability risk has risen to substantial.\nImplementation challenges have increased, affecting planning, management, budgeting, and monitoring and\nevaluation. Turnover of government staff, limited experience with international financial institutions,\nprocess-intensive implementation, regulatory and bureaucratic hurdles, and procurement delays have\nincreased transaction times, requiring the restructuring of implementation arrangements in several projects.\nTo mitigate these risks, IBRD has strengthened the coordination with government, created early warning\nmechanisms across the portfolio, and enhanced implementation readiness on new investment projects.\n49. Insecurity, crime, and violence continue to pose substantial risks to CPF implementation. Security\nchallenges affect beneficiaries and staff, impede mission travel and delay operations. It also recorded proactive restructurings, but noted that changing priorities, budget constraints, institutional turnover, procurement delays, and limited implementation capacity affected continuity and results.", "question_ids": ["5B.7", "8B.6", "8B.8"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly covers the FY2020-2023 implementation record relevant to the end of López Obrador's 2023 period.", "ruler_attribution": "The review explicitly records government-requested cancellations and changing priorities, but also identifies World Bank and institutional factors.", "limitations": ["The record concerns the World Bank portfolio, not all federal projects.", "Cancellation can reflect adaptation or failure; the document gives differing project-specific reasons."], "start_unit": 70, "end_unit": 75, "locator": "page 70 through page 75", "exact_excerpt": "Annex 5. Program Implementation\nProgram and Portfolio Performance\n1. World Bank lending to Mexico has increased. As of December 2023, the World Bank active\nportfolio includes US$1.68 billion from the International Bank for Reconstruction and Development\n(IBRD), US$2.4 billion committed portfolio from the International Finance Corporation (IFC), and US$536\nmillion from the Multilateral Investment Guarantee Agency (MIGA). Under the current CPF, the total\nIBRD lending approved sums up to US$4.3 billion (US$1.1 billion average per year) relative to US$560.0\nmillion in the previous CPF. The International Finance Corporation (IFC) also committed investments\nincreased in the first four years relative to the same period of the previous CPF, from US$1.62 billion\nduring FY14-17 to US$1.75 billion during FY20-23. Responding to global context and increased client\nappetite for countercyclical financing sources, DPF increased to US$3.9 billion compared to US$0.5 billion\nunder the previous CPF.228 Over FY20-FY23, four Development Policy Financing (DPF) operations were\nextended to the Mexican authorities, three directly supporting the COVID-19 crisis response and\nrecovery, including IBRD’s first Build Back Better DPF. For FY24, the pipeline for the remaining CPF\nconsists of only one DPF project. In addition, IBRD has an active Bank-executed trust fund portfolio\nbenefiting the country which comprises 34 trust funds for a total net commitment of US$71.1 million.\n2. IBRD portfolio has moved from an average of sixteen projects during the previous CPF to ten\nprojects during the current CPF, with a gradual reduction of investment project financing (IPF). IPF was\nreduced from 11 projects in 2020 to 7 in 2023. The active IBRD portfolio as of December 2023, comprises\nfive IBRD loans (US$1.6 billion), complemented by two Global Environment Facility (GEF) projects, and\none recipient executed trust-funded project. Since the CPF was discussed by the Board, seven IBRD\nprojects and one GEF stand-alone project have been approved. During this same period, ten projects\nclosed, four operations were partially cancelled, four were prepared but dropped at later stages of\npreparation, and two were not signed resulting in withdrawn loan offers (Table 5.2).\nTable 5.1. Restructurings have been a key component of portfolio management, including: (i) partial\ncancellations (e.g., US$100 million of the Energy Efficiency in Public Facilities Project and US$45 million of\nthe Modernization of Public Financial Management Systems in Mexico Project); (ii) extensions of closing\ndates (including in half of the total restructurings to date – eleven extensions from FY20-23); (iii)\nadjustment to the Results Framework as well as changes to components and costs, are some of the\nproactive actions taken to address implementation bottlenecks.\n66\n\nTable 5.4.
